                                             FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS

                                                                 WALTER GUBLER, PHILIPP JELL, AND JOSEPH RABINOFF
arXiv:2111.05741v3 [math.AG] 7 Mar 2025




                                                  ABSTRACT. We introduce tropical skeletons for Berkovich spaces based on results of Duc-
                                                  ros. Then we study harmonic functions on good strictly analytic spaces over a non-trivially
                                                  valued non-Archimedean field. Chambert-Loir and Ducros introduced bigraded sheaves
                                                  of smooth real-valued differential forms on Berkovich spaces by pulling back Lagerberg
                                                  forms with respect to tropicalization maps. We give a new approach in which we allow
                                                  pullback by more general harmonic tropicalizations to get a larger sheaf of differential
                                                  forms with essentially the same properties, but with a better cohomological behavior. A
                                                  crucial ingredient is that tropical varieties arising from harmonic tropicalization maps are
                                                  balanced.

                                         1. INTRODUCTION
   Arakelov theory is an arithmetic intersection theory on arithmetic varieties. The con-
tributions of the Archimedean places are described analytically, while intersection theory
on models over valuation rings is used for the non-Archimedean places. It is an old dream
to handle all places simultaneously in an analytic manner. This would have the advan-
tage of replacing the models, which sometimes do not exist in sufficient generality, by
the underlying Berkovich analytic spaces.
   It is here that tropical geometry becomes very helpful. Lagerberg [Lag12] has intro-
duced smooth (p, q)-forms on Rn given in terms of coordinates by
                           X
                    α=          α I J d′ x i1 ∧ · · · ∧ d′ x ip ∧ d′′ x j1 ∧ · · · ∧ d′′ x jq
                        |I |=p,|J |=q

where I (resp. J) consists of i1 < · · · < ip (resp. j1 < · · · < jq ) and α I J are C ∞ -functions
on Rn . The smooth Lagerberg forms give rise to a bigraded differential sheaf A•,• of
R-algebras on Rn with alternating product ∧ and with natural differentials d′ and d′′
(see 2.7). They are the analogues of the differential operators ∂ and ∂ in complex anal-
ysis.
   One defines smooth Lagerberg forms on a weighted rational polyhedral complex (Π, m)
in Rn by restriction (see 2.7). This is analogous to the definition of differential forms on
singular complex analytic spaces. In applications, (Π, m) will be a tropical variety. One
has integrals and boundary integrals over (Π, m) satisfying the formula of Stokes.
   From now on, we consider a good, d-dimensional strictly analytic Berkovich space X
over a non-trivially valued non-Archimedean field K. For the basic notions used from
non-archimedean geometry, we refer to §2.3. For a compact analytic subdomain W of
X , a smooth tropicalization map F : W → Rn is given by F (x) = (− log | f i (x)|)i=1,...,n for
invertible analytic functions f i on W . Chambert-Loir and Ducros [CD12] used smooth
tropicalization maps to lift smooth Lagerberg forms to X . In this way, they obtained a
bigraded differential sheaf A•,•
                               sm
                                  of R-algebras on X with alternating product ∧ and with
                       ′      ′′
natural differentials d and d (see 2.8).
   This opens the door to the dream mentioned above. Many results hold as expected
from complex geometry. Chambert-Loir and Ducros proved that there are analogues of
smooth partitions of unity, of the formula of Stokes and of the Poincaré-Lelong formula.
The d′′ -operator induces an analogue of the Dolbeault complex and we can introduce the
tropical Dolbeault cohomology by
                                                 d′′
(1.0.1)             p,q
                   Hsm               p,q
                        (X ) := ker(Asm           p,q+1
                                         (X ) −→ Asm    (X ))/d′′ (Asm
                                                                    p,q−1
                                                                          (X )).

It was shown in [Jel16] that the sheaves Asm  p,q
                                                  satisfy a local Poincaré Lemma. If X = Y an
for a separated smooth scheme Y of finite type over K, then Liu [Liu20] showed that
there is an analogue of the cycle class map. However, it was realized in [Jel19b] that the
                       1,1
cohomology groups Hsm      (X ) can be infinite dimensional and that Poincaré duality can
fail for smooth projective curves X over K. The main problem can be already found in
Thuillier’s thesis [Thu05] and is a result of the fact that not all harmonic functions on
curves are smooth, as explained in Wanner’s master thesis [Wan16].
              FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                  3

   It was suggested in [Jel19a] that we can overcome these problems by using more
general tropicalization maps based on harmonic functions. The present paper gives the
foundations of such a theory. With this approach, we will get a larger bigraded differential
sheaf A•,• of R-algebras on X consisting of weakly smooth forms. The weakly smooth
forms have all the nice properties of smooth forms, but they have a better cohomological
behavior.
   The notation and prerequisites for this paper are explained in Section 2. We will use
smooth forms and currents introduced in [CD12]. Some of their definitions are recalled
later as a special case of our more general construction.

1.1. Tropical Skeletons and Tropical Multiplicities. In Section 3, we assume that X
is compact and of pure dimension d, and we consider a morphism ϕ : X → Tan , where
        n
T = Gm    is an n-dimensional split torus over K. In case d = n, Ducros [Duc12] has
defined the skeleton Sϕ (X ) as the preimage of the canonical skeleton of Tan , and he has
shown that the skeleton has the structure of a piecewise linear space only depending on
its underlying set and the analytic structure of X . Using Temkin’s graded reductions, we
will generalize this construction and define a tropical skeleton Sϕ (X ) for arbitrary n. By
Proposition 3.12, it is a closed subset of X consisting only of Abhyankar points, and it
depends only on the smooth tropicalization map F : X → Rn induced by ϕ. We will see
in Remark 3.20 that the tropical skeleton Sϕ (X ) is a piecewise linear space of dimension
at most d closely related to the tropical variety F (X ). Indeed, the restriction of F induces
a surjective piecewise linear map Sϕ (X ) → F (X ) with finite fibers. We will show in
Remark 3.19 that Sϕ (X ) coincides with the tropical skeleton from [GRW16] studied in
the algebraic case. In Section 4, we will define and study positive tropical multiplicities
mϕ (X , x) at x ∈ Sϕ (X ) and mϕ (X , ω) at ω ∈ F (X ). The latter generalize the classical
tropical multiplicities studied in tropical geometry.

1.2. Formal and Semipositive Metrics. Let L be a line bundle on a paracompact good
strictly analytic space X over K. We consider a formal K ◦ -model (X, L) of (X , L), i.e. an
admissible formal scheme X with generic fiber X and a line bundle L on X with L|X = L.
Then we have an associated formal metric k kL on L characterized by kskL ≡ 1 for any
trivializing local section s of L (see [GM19, Section 2] for details). If we can choose L
such that the restriction of L to the special fiber Xs is nef, then we call k kL semipositive.
   Let L be a line bundle on a separated good strictly analytic space X over K. We assume
that the boundary ∂ X is empty, which holds for example if X is algebraic. The first Chern
current c1 (L, k k) of a continuous metric k k on L is the current on X defined locally by
d′ d′′ [− log ksk], where s is a local trivializing section of L and [ f ] denotes the current
associated to a continuous function f . As in complex analysis, this does not depend
on the choice of s and hence defines a current on X (see [CD12, 6.4.1]). We call such
a current positive if it is non-negative on compactly supported smooth positive forms
(see 2.8). For i ∈ N, let k ki be a metric of L such that for every x ∈ X there is a strictly
                                                                                ⊗n
affinoid neighbourhood W and nW ∈ N>0 such that the restriction of k ki W to W is a
semipositive formal metric. As formal metrics are continuous, we conclude that k ki is
a continuous metric on L.
4                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

Theorem 1.3. Under the above assumptions, assume that the metrics k ki converge uni-
formly to the metric k k of L. Then the first Chern current c1 (L, k k) is positive.
  Such metrics play an important role for the contributions of non-Archimedean places in
Arakelov theory and were originally introduced by Zhang [Zha95] in an algebraic frame-
work. We will prove Theorem 1.3 in Remark 7.16 based on the results from Sections 5–7.
1.4. Piecewise Linear and Harmonic Functions. In Section 5, we will study piecewise
Λ-linear functions on a good strictly analytic space X over K for a subgroup Λ of R.
Roughly, these are piecewise linear functions with slopes contained in Λ.
Proposition 1.5. Assume that Λ = Z or that Λ is a divisible group containing 1.
    (1) The piecewise Λ-linear functions form a sheaf of Λ-modules on the G-topology of X ,
        which is generated by the sheaf of piecewise Z-linear functions (Remark 5.2).
    (2) Piecewise Λ-linearity is stable under pull-back (Lemma 5.4).
    (3) If X is paracompact, then h: X → R is piecewise Z-linear if and only if there is a
        formal K ◦ -model (X, L) of (X , OX ) with h = − log k1kL (see 5.6).
    (4) Property (1) holds also with respect to the analytic topology of X (Proposition 5.8).
    (5) Piecewise Λ-linearity is stable under extension K ′ /K of the ground field (Lemma 5.4).
    (6) If K ′ /K is normal in (5), then base change gives precisely the Aut(K ′ /K)-invariant
        piecewise Λ-linear functions on X ⊗ b K K ′ (Corollary 5.13).
    (7) A real function h: X → R is piecewise Z-linear if and only if h is locally the composi-
        tion of a smooth tropicalization map with a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear function on the
        corresponding tropical variety (Proposition 8.3).
Properties (1), (2) and (3) characterize piecewise Λ-linear functions. We need the assump-
tion 1 ∈ Λ to ensure that the piecewise Z-linear functions are piecewise Λ-linear, which is
used in (1).
  In Section 6, we recall Temkin’s reduction of germs, then we define the residue of
a piecewise Λ-linear function at a point x ∈ X as a canonical invertible sheaf on the
reduction of the germ at x, following [CD12, Section 6]. This is an important tool used in
Section 7, where we will explain the notion of harmonic functions. For non-Archimedean
curves, they were investigated in Thuillier’s thesis [Thu05]. In any dimension, we can
describe harmonic functions on good strictly analytic spaces as follows.
Proposition 1.6. Assume that Λ = Z or that Λ is a divisible group containing 1.
   (1) Every Λ-harmonic function is piecewise Λ-linear (Definition 7.2).
   (2) The Λ-harmonic functions form a sheaf of Λ-modules generated by the sheaf of Z-
       harmonic functions (Remark 7.3, Proposition 7.5).
   (3) The Λ-harmonic functions are stable under pull-back (Proposition 7.6).
   (4) A function h: X → R is Z-harmonic if and only if every x ∈ X has a strictly affinoid
       neighbourhood W and a formal K ◦ -model (W, L) of (W, OW ) with h|W = − log k1kL
       such that L restricts to a numerically trivial line bundle on Xs (Proposition 7.1).
   (5) An R-harmonic function is Λ-harmonic if and only if it is piecewise Λ-linear (see 7.3).
   (6) The Λ-harmonic functions are stable under base extension K ′ /K (Proposition 7.6).
   (7) If K ′ /K is normal in (6), then base change gives precisely the Aut(K ′ /K)-invariant
       Λ-harmonic functions on X ⊗   b K K ′ (Proposition 7.7).
              FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                 5

   (8) On a rig-smooth curve, a function is R-harmonic if and only if it is harmonic in the
       sense of Thuillier (Proposition 15.7).
Properties (2), (3) and (4) characterize Λ-harmonic functions.
  In a subsequent paper, we will show that the maximum principle holds on X \ ∂ X for
Λ-harmonic functions.

1.7. Piecewise Linear and Harmonic Tropicalizations. For a compact strictly analytic
domain W of X of pure dimension d, we say that h: W → Rn is a piecewise linear
(resp. harmonic) tropicalization if the induced coordinate functions hi : W → R are piece-
wise Z-linear (resp. Z-harmonic). We will show in Section 8 that for every piecewise
linear tropicalization h: W → Rn we get canonical tropical weights on the d-dimensional
part Troph (W )d := h(W )d of the tropical variety Troph (W ) := h(W ). The latter is a union
of rational polytopes in Rn of dimension at most d. The main idea is that piecewise linear
tropicalizations are locally covered by smooth tropicalization maps for which we can use
the tropical weights from Section 4. Note that smooth tropicalization maps are in partic-
ular harmonic tropicalizations; in this case the tropical multiplicities agree with the ones
from 1.1. For a piecewise linear tropicalization h, we will define an associated d-skeleton
Sh (W )d as a canonical compact piecewise linear space in W associated to h, and we will
endow its d-dimensional faces with canonical positive tropical weights such that
(1.7.1)                          h∗ (Sh (W )d ) = Troph (W )d
as an identity of weighted polytopal complexes in Rn . We will show that the underly-
ing weighted polytopal complexes satisfy a functoriality property generalizing the well-
known Sturmfels–Tevelev formula from tropical geometry.

1.8. The Balancing Condition. It turns out that the key property of the smooth tropi-
calization maps used in [CD12] is that tropical varietes are balanced outside the image
of the boundary; this is more or less equivalent to the formula of Stokes. It is now crucial
to extend this to harmonic tropicalizations.
Theorem 1.9. Let h: W → Rn be a harmonic tropicalization on a compact strictly ana-
lytic subdomain W of X of pure dimension d. Then the canonical tropical weights satisfy
the balancing condition in every (d − 1)-dimensional face of Troph (W ) not contained in
h(Sh (W )d ∩∂ W ).
   The balancing condition will be explained in Section 9, and Theorem 1.9 will be proven
in Theorem 9.7 by first showing a local version for a germ (X , x) at x ∈ X . The balancing
theorem generalizes the well-known fact from tropical geometry that tropical varieties of
closed subschemes of a split torus over K are balanced. Note that if W = X is algebraic,
no boundary ∂ W occurs. Theorem 1.9 is also a generalization of [CD12, Theorem 3.6.1],
where the statement is shown for smooth tropicalization maps. We use this to show first
a local version for smooth germs of tropicalization maps, then we will use that every har-
monic tropicalization is locally covered by a smooth tropicalization map, and finally we
will employ a trick with the graph to descend the balancing condition. Here, Theorem 1.3
and the tropical Poincaré–Lelong formula are applied.
6                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

1.10. Weakly Smooth Forms. In Section 10, we will introduce weakly smooth forms on
good strictly analytic spaces over K. The idea is to lift Lagerberg forms by using harmonic
tropicalizations, in analogy with the definition of smooth forms [CD12]. The properties
of weakly smooth forms are summarized as follows:
Theorem 1.11. There is a bigraded differential sheaf A•,• of R-algebras on X with an al-
ternating product ∧ and differentials d′ , d′′ whose elements are called weakly smooth forms.
These sheaves satisfy the following properties:
    (1) For a morphism f : X ′ → X , there is a homomorphism f ∗ : AX → f∗ AX ′ of sheaves
        of bigraded differential R-algebras. This pull-back is functorial (see 10.8).
    (2) If X is compact with a harmonic tropicalization map h: X → Rn , there is an injective
        homomorphism h∗ : A•,• (h(X )) → A•,• (X ) of bigraded differential R-algebras lifting
        smooth Lagerberg forms on the tropical variety h(X ) to X (Proposition 10.9).
    (3) If X , h are as in (2) and f : X ′ → X is a morphism of compact good strictly analytic
        spaces over K, then (h ◦ f )∗ = f ∗ ◦ h∗ (Proposition 10.9).
    (4) For ω ∈ A(X ), any x ∈ X has a strictly affinoid neighbourhood W with a har-
        monic tropicalization h: W → Rn such that ω|W = h∗ (α) for some α ∈ A(h(W ))
        (see 10.7).
    (5) The sheaf A•,•sm
                          of smooth forms on X introduced by Chambert-Loir and Ducros is a
        subsheaf of bigraded differential R-algebras of A•,• (Proposition 10.14).
    (6) The elements of A0,0 (X ) are continuous functions on X . A real function f on X is
        R-harmonic if and only if f ∈ A0,0 (X ) and d′ d′′ f = 0 (Proposition 10.17).
The sheaves A•,• of bigraded differential R-algebras are characterized up to unique isomor-
phism by properties (1), (2), (3) and (4).
1.12. Integration and Strong Currents. In Section 11, we give a theory of integration
for weakly smooth forms. Since weakly smooth forms are piecewise smooth, this can be
easily deduced from the integration theory for smooth forms in [CD12] and the inclusion-
exclusion formula. We will prove the formulae of Stokes (Theorem 11.3) and Green
(Theorem 11.5) for weakly smooth forms.
   In Section 12, we will introduce strong currents on a pure dimensional separated
strictly analytic space X with no boundary as a topologically dual notion of compactly
supported weakly smooth forms. As the boundary of X is empty, the balancing condi-
tion in Theorem 1.9 yields that the boundary integral in the formula of Stokes vanishes,
and hence we can embed the space of weakly smooth forms into the space of strong cur-
rents. We will show that the Poincaré–Lelong formula holds in the sense of strong currents
(Theorem 12.7).
1.13. Dolbeault Cohomology and Curves. The Dolbeault complex 0 → A p,0 (X ) →
· · · → A p,d (X ) → 0 for d′′ induces the Dolbeault cohomology groups H p,q (X ), now us-
ing the sheaf of weakly smooth forms A and not the sheaf Asm of smooth forms as in
(1.0.1). We will show in Section 13 that this new Dolbeault cohomology satisfies also a
local Poincaré Lemma (Theorem 13.1) and we will give an analogue of Liu’s cycle class map
for this new Dolbeault cohomology with the same properties as before (Theorem 13.6).
     To see that H p,q (X ) is better behaved than Hsm
                                                    p,q
                                                        (X ) from (1.0.1), we consider a compact
                                    p,q
rig-smooth curve X . Then H (X ) has the (finite) dimension expected from the case of
               FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                     7

compact Riemann surfaces, and Poincaré duality holds if X is proper. This will be shown
in our companion paper [GJR21]. As a special case, we mention here the following result:
Theorem 1.14. Let Y be a smooth projective curve over K and let b the first Betti number
of X = Y an . Then dim(H p,q (X )) is equal to 1 (resp. b) if p + q ∈ {0, 2} (resp. if p + q = 1).
   The idea is to introduce (p, q)-forms on a weighted metric graph with boundary and
to compute the Dolbeault cohomology first in this combinatorial setting. We will then
show that if K ′ /K is a finite Galois extension such that X ′ := X ⊗K K ′ admits a strictly
semistable model, then H p,q (X ′ ) is canonically isomorphic to the Dolbeault cohomology
of its associated skeleton. By a Galois descent argument, the theorem follows. Although
Theorem 1.14 will be proven in [GJR21], it relies on the foundations developed here. In
particular, we will use the results about the Galois action on weakly smooth forms and
on Dolbeault cohomology discussed in Section 14.
   In Section 15, we will restrict ourselves to the case of a rig-smooth curve. We will show
that our notion of harmonic functions agrees with Thuillier’s notion from [Thu05]. If the
curve has a strictly semistable model, then we will compare harmonic functions on the
curve to harmonic functions on the skeleton. At the end, we will give a regularization
result (Theorem 15.11) for subharmonic functions on a rig-smooth curve generalizing a
theorem of Wanner.
1.15. Canonical Tropicalizations for Abelian Varieties. In Section 16, we will show
that for an abelian variety A over K, the canonical tropicalization map trop: Aan → NR is
a harmonic tropicalization. Here, we denote by N the cocharacter lattice of the torus T
involved in Raynaud’s uniformization theory for A . In Section 17, we will give an example
of an abelian threefold with mixed reduction such that the canonical tropicalization is not
a smooth tropicalization map. This shows that weakly smooth forms arise very naturally
for abelian varieties.
   Let MR = Hom(N , R) and Λ p,q MR := Λ p MR ⊗R Λq MR be the bigraded exterior algebra of
MR . Then there is a canonical injective homomorphism Λ p,q MR → H p,q (A) for all p, q ∈ N.
This was shown by Miriam Prechtel in her thesis [Pre23] based on results of this paper.
We do not know if this is an isomorphism as in the complex case.
1.16. Acknowledgments. We thank the participants of the Oberseminar Arakelov The-
ory in the summer term 2021 for useful comments about an earlier draft of the paper
and Andreas Mihatsch for his remarks on the first arXiv version and the referee for the
suggestions to improve the presentation of the paper.

                                      2. PREREQUISITES
2.1. General Notation and Conventions. The set of natural numbers N includes 0.p A
set-theoretic inclusion S ⊂ T allows equality. For an additive subgroup Λ ⊂ R we let Λ
denote its divisible hull. If P is any abelian group, we let PΛ := P ⊗Z Λ be the tensor
product with Λ.
   Rings are always assumed to be commutative and with 1. For a ring A, the group of
invertible elements is denoted by A× . A variety over a field F is an integral scheme which
is of finite type and separated over Spec F .
8                             W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

  A space is compact if it is quasi-compact and Hausdorff. A paracompact space is a
Hausdorff space such that every open covering has a locally finite open refinement.
2.2. Non-Archimedean Fields. Throughout the whole paper, we fix a field K that is
complete with respect to a non-trivial non-Archimedean complete absolute value de-
noted | |: K → R. We will call this a non-trivially valued non-Archimedean field. A non-
Archimedean extension field is a non-Archimedean field K ′ equipped with an isometric
embedding K ,→ K ′ .
   We will make use of Temkin’s theory of graded residue fields introduced in [Tem04];
see also [Duc24, 2.2] and [Duc12, Section 0]. We use K  e to denote the ordinary residue

2.3. Non-Archimedean geometry. We will use Berkovich analytic spaces as defined
in [Ber93]. We are mainly concerned with good analytic spaces X over a non-trivially
valued non-Archimedean field K. These are the analytic spaces considered in [Ber90], so
this book is our main reference for non-Archimedean geometry. Here, good means that
every x ∈ V has a neighbourhood isomorphic to the Berkovich spectrum M (A ) of an
affinoid algebra A .
   When considering strictly analytic spaces, we will always use the G-topology generated
by the strictly affinoid domains [Ber93, 1.3]. This is most natural for comparison to rigid
analytic geometry: see [Ber93, 1.6].
We will use the following notation concerning Berkovich analytic spaces. Let X be an
analytic space and let A be a K-affinoid algebra.

  Note that X an is good strictly analytic space over K with empty boundary.

2.4. Formal geometry and models. We will make extensive use of admissible formal
models of analytic spaces. We refer the reader to [Bos14, BL93a] for the definitions.
We will use the following notation concerning admissible formal schemes. Let X be an
admissible formal K ◦ -scheme, i.e., a flat formal K ◦ -scheme locally of topological finite
type such that X has a locally finite atlas by affine formal schemes.

          Xη The generic fiber of X, a strictly K-analytic space.
                                       e -scheme locally of finite type.
          Xs The special fiber of X, a K
        redX : Xη → Xs , the functorial reduction map.
   An affine admissible formal K ◦ -scheme is the formal spectrum Spf(A) of a K ◦ -algebra A
which is topologically of finite type and flat over K ◦ . The generic fiber of Spf(A) is defined
as the spectrum of the K-affinoid algebra A ⊗K ◦ K; this construction globalizes under the
above assumptions to give the generic fiber of X. If X is separated, then it follows from
our above finiteness assumption on the atlas and from [Ber93, Theorem 1.6.1] that the
generic fiber Xη is a paracompact strictly analytic space. Below we will see a converse.
   Let X be a good paracompact strictly analytic space over K. A formal K ◦ -model X of X
is an admissible formal K ◦ -scheme X with an identification Xη = X . We say that a formal
K ◦ -model X′ dominates X if there exists a morphism X′ → X inducing the identity on
generic fibers. A theorem of Raynaud states that the set of isomorphism classes of K ◦ -
models of X is non-empty and filtered with respect to the dominance relation: see [Bos14,
8.4] and [GM19, 2.4]. A paracompact space is Hausdorff, and as X is also good, it follows
that X is a separated Berkovich space [Ber90, Proposition 3.1.5]. Hence we see that every
formal K ◦ -model of X is separated by using [BL93a, Proposition 4.7].
   If K is algebraically closed and X is reduced, then for any formal K ◦ -model X of X there
is a dominating K ◦ -model X′ of X with reduced special fiber and with X′ → X finite. There
10                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

is a minimal K ◦ -model with these properties. It is given over a formal affine open subset
Spf(A) by Spf((A ⊗K ◦ K)◦ ). See [Gub98, 1.10] and [GM19, Proof of Proposition 3.5].
   A formal K ◦ -model X of X can be modified by an admissible formal blowing up. This is
a dominating morphism π: X′ → X obtained by a blowing up a center supported in Xs .
See [Bos14, 8.2] for details.
   Let L be a line bundle on X , i.e., a locally free OX -module of rank 1. A formal K ◦ -
model (X, L) of (X , L) is a line bundle L on a formal K ◦ -model X of X together with an
identification L|X = L.

2.5. Polyhedral geometry. In this subsection, we collect the notions from convex geom-
etry that we will use throughout the paper. We suggest that the reader browse through
this subsection in a first read-through to become familiar with our conventions regarding
polyhedra, polytopes, polyhedral complexes, subcomplexes, and fans, as there are sev-
eral competing definitions used in the literature. At a later stage, one might come back
to this subsection if needed.
   Usually, we have the following setup for convex geometry.
           N A lattice, i.e. a free Z-module of finite rank.
          NR = N ⊗ R, the ambient real vector space.
          NΓ = N ⊗ Γ for an additive subgroup Γ ⊂ R.
           M = Hom(N , Z), the dual lattice.
         MR = Hom(N , R), the dual vector space.
         MΓ = Hom(N , Γ ) for an additive subgroup Γ ⊂ R.
     〈 · , · 〉 : MR × NR → R, the evaluation pairing.
   Now we recall some notions from convex geometry in NR . These notions are often
introduced in the special case of the lattice Zn inside Rn . We fix a subring R of R and an
R-submodule Γ of R. In applications, we use this mainly for R = Z and Γ = v(K × ).
   An affine function on NR is a function of the form the form u + c with u ∈ MR and c ∈ R.
We say that the affine function is (R, Γ )-linear if u ∈ MR and c ∈ Γ . For another lattice N ′ ,
we say that a function L : NR′ → NR is (R, Γ )-linear if f ◦ L is (R, Γ )-linear on NR′ for each
(R, Γ )-linear function f : NR → R.
   In the above and what follows, we abbreviate the prefix “(R, R)-” (in which no condition
is required of the translation) simply by “R-”
   A polyhedron σ in NR is a finite intersection of half-spaces H i := { f i ≥ 0} for affine
functions f i on NR . If we can choose all f i to be (R, Γ )-linear, then we say that σ is an
(R, Γ )-polyhedron. A polytope is a bounded polyhedron. A face of a polyhedron σ is the
intersection of σ with the boundary of a half-space H ⊃ σ. We also allow σ and ; as
faces of σ.
   A polyhedral complex in NR is a finite collection Π of polyhedra such that for any σ ∈ Π,
all faces of σ are contained in Π, and such that for σ, ρ ∈ Π, the intersection σ ∩ ρ is a
face of both σ and ρ. A (R, Γ )-polyhedral complex is a polyhedral complex consisting of
                 S If all σ ∈ Π are polytopes, then Π is called a polytopal complex. The
(R, Γ )-polyhedra.
support |Π| := σ∈Π σ of a polytopal complex Π is called a polytopal set.
   A subcomplex of Π is a polyhedral complex Π′ ⊂ Π A subdivision of Π is a polyhedral
complex Π′′ with |Π| = |Π′′ |, such that each σ ∈ Π is a union of polyhedra in Π′′ .
               FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                  11

   A (rational) fan in NR is a (Z, {0})-polyhedral complex. It consists of Z-polyhedral
cones centered at 0. For a Z-polyhedral complex Π in NR and ω ∈ |Π|, we define the star
around ω as {σ ∈ Π | ω ∈ σ}. By translation to the origin, the star generates a fan in NR .
   For a Z-polyhedron σ in NR , let Lσ be the R-linear subspace of NR generated by {ω1 −
ω2 | ω1 , ω2 ∈ σ}. Note that Lσ is generated by the saturated lattice Nσ := N ∩ Lσ .
   A weighted polyhedral complex of dimension d is a polyhedral complex Π in NR such
that any maximal σ ∈ Π is d-dimensional and comes with a weight mσ ∈ Z. We consider
weighted (R, Γ )-polyhedral complexes (Π, m) and (Π′ , m′ ) to be equivalent if we can re-
place them by (R, Γ )-subdivisions such that they agree outside the weight zero part. More
precisely, this means that there is a subdivision Π′′ of Π such that for every d-dimensional
face σ of Π′′ contained in some ∆ ∈ Π with m∆ 6= 0, there exists ∆′ ∈ Π′ with σ ⊂ ∆′ and
m∆′ = m∆ , and the same holds with the role of Π and Π′ reversed. Equivalence classes
of weighted (R, Γ )-polyhedral complexes form an abelian group by adding the weights of
a joint (R, Γ )-subdivision. The zero element is given by 0 := (NR , 0).
   An abstract polyhedral complex is a finite collection Π of polyhedra satisfying the same
conditions as above, where each σ ∈ Π is a full-dimensional polyhedron in a real vector
space Rnσ , but where we do not require that all polyhedra be contained in the same
ambient space Rn . We call Π an abstract (R, Γ )-polyhedral complex if for a face τ of
σ ∈ Π, the induced affine map Rnτ → Rnσ is (R, Γ )-linear. An (R, Γ )-piecewise linear space
is the support of an abstract polyhedral complex, and the (R, Γ )-piecewise linear structure
is given by the polyhedra of Π, well-defined up to (R, Γ )-subdivison.
   A function L : S ′ → S between piecewise (R, Γ )-linear spaces S, S ′ is a piecewise (R, Γ )-
linear map if there exist polyhedral complexes Π, Π′ with |Π| = S, |Π′ | = S ′ such that
for every ρ ∈ Π′ , there is σ ∈ Π with L(ρ) = σ, and L|ρ induces an (R, Γ )-linear map
Rnρ → Rnσ .
   We are mainly concerned with piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear maps f : |Π| → R on a weighted
(Z, Γ )-polyhedral complex (Π, m) of dimension d. Such functions play the role of rational
functions in tropical intersection theory. We have an associated tropical Weil divisor div( f )
which is a (d − 1)-dimensional weighted (Z, Γ )-polyhedral subcomplex of a subdivision
of Π. See [AR10, Section 6].
   Let S be a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear space. A weighted abstract (Z, Γ )-polyhedral complex
of dimension d is given by endowing the d-dimensional faces ∆ of an abstract polyhedral
complex Π defining S with weights m∆ ∈ Z. The resulting weighted polyhedral complex
is denoted by (Πd , m). As above, weighted abstract polyhedral complexes are equivalent
if they agree after subdivision. For a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear map L : S → S ′ of piecewise
(Z, Γ )-linear spaces, the push-forward L∗ (Πd , m) is a weighted (Z, Γ )-polyhedral complex
of dimension d on S ′ , well-defined up to equivalence. See [AR10, Section 7].

2.6. Tori and tropicalizations. Let T be an n-dimensional split torus over the non-
Archimedean field K, and let N be the cocharacter lattice of T. Then the tropicalization
map is the function trop: Tan → NR defined as follows. For u in the character lattice
M = Hom(N , Z), let χ u be the corresponding character of T = Spec(K[M ]). Then the
image trop(x) ∈ NR of x ∈ Tan is characterized by
                                 〈u, trop(x)〉 = − log |χ u (x)|
12                                W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

for all u ∈ M . If we choose toric coordinates x 1 , . . . , x n on T identifying N with Zn , then
trop(x) = (− log |x 1 (x)|, . . . , − log |x n(x)|) under the identification NR ∼ = Rn . Working
analytically has the advantage that trop is a continuous proper map.
   There is a canonical section ι : NR → Tan = Spec(K[M ])an of the tropicalization map
trop: Tan → NR defined by
                            X                         
(2.6.1)             − log         au ξu          = min v(au ) + 〈u, ω〉 | au 6= 0 .
                            u∈M           ι(ω)

Its image is the canonical skeleton S(T) of T, defined in [Ber90, 6.3].
   Given an analytic space X , a morphism ϕ : X → Tan is called a moment map. We let
ϕtrop = trop ◦ϕ : X → NR be the corresponding smooth tropicalization map.

We will use the following notations concerning tropical geometry and tori.
        T A split torus over K.
       M The character lattice of T.
       N The cocharacter lattice of T.
       ξu The character of T corresponding to u ∈ M .
    trop : Tan → NR , the tropicalization map.
        ι : NR → Tan , the section of trop.
    S(T) = ι(NR ), the canonical skeleton of T.
    ϕtrop = trop ◦ϕ, the smooth tropicalization map associated to ϕ : X → Tan .

2.7. Lagerberg forms. Let N be a lattice of rank n. Lagerberg [Lag12] has introduced
the sheaf A p,q := A p ⊗C ∞ Aq of (p, q)-forms on NR , where on the right we use the sheaf A•
of usual smooth differential forms on NR . Such (p, q)-forms are called smooth Lagerberg
forms. As in complex geometry, the smooth Lagerberg forms give rise to a bigraded dif-
ferential sheaf A•,• of R-algebras with an alternating product ∧ and natural differentials
d′ : A p,q → A p+1,q and d′′ : A p,q → A p,q+1.
    The Lagerberg involution is the unique involution J of the sheaf A of R-algebras which
leaves the smooth functions fixed and which satisfies Jd′ = d′′ J. It maps A p,q isomorphi-
cally onto Aq,p . For an open subset Ω of NR , we call ω ∈ A p,p (Ω) a positive Lagerberg form
if there are α1 , . . . , αm ∈ A p,0 (Ω) and smooth functions f j : Ω → R≥0 with

                                        3. TROPICAL SKELETONS
   Let X be a compact strictly analytic space over K of pure dimension d. We consider a
moment map ϕ : X → Tan to a split torus T over K, and we let N and M be the cocharacter
and character lattices of T, respectively. Note that the moment map ϕ is equivalent to a
homomorphism M → Γ (X , OX× ). We will use results of Ducros [Duc12] to attach to ϕ a
tropical skeleton in X which generalizes the algebraic case considered in [GRW16].
   In fact, the assumption that X is a strictly analytic space was done only for simplicity
of notation and can be easily generalized to Γ -strictly analytic space for any subgroup Γ
of R containing the value group of K as defined in [Duc12, 0.20].
Definition 3.1. The tropical variety of ϕ : X → Tan is the image ϕtrop (X ) ⊂ NR .
3.2 (Polyhedral Structure on Tropicalizations). A result of Berkovich, which might be
seen as an analytic version of the Bieri–Groves theorem, shows that that the tropical
variety ϕtrop (X ) is the support of a (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex Π in NR of dimension at
most d. A theorem of Ducros [Duc12, Theorem 3.2] shows that we may choose Π such
that ϕtrop (∂ X ) is contained in a subcomplex of dimension at most d − 1. If X is strictly
affinoid, then ϕtrop (∂ X ) is equal to the support of such a subcomplex.
                FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                            15

3.3 (Tropicalizations of Germs). Ducros [Duc12, Theorem 3.4] has also shown that for
every x ∈ X , there is a compact strictly analytic neighbourhood V (x) of x such that for
all compact strictly analytic neighbourhoods W ⊂ V (x) of x, the germs of the tropical
varieties ϕtrop (W ) and ϕtrop (V (x)) agree at ϕtrop (x).
Definition 3.4. The tropical variety ϕtrop (X , x) of a germ (X , x) is the germ of ϕtrop (V (x))
at ϕtrop (x). The tropical dimension of (X , x) is dim ϕtrop (X , x).
3.5. The tropical variety of a germ (X , x) is the germ of a fan at ϕtrop (x). This germ may
be strictly smaller than the germ of ϕtrop (X ) at ϕtrop (x). For example, the tropical variety
of the germ of X at a rigid point is always a point. More generally, the tropical dimension
of (X , x) is bounded in Theorem 3.10 below.
  In the following, we will use graded residue fields introduced in 2.2. Recall from 2.3
that χ         f(x)• is the canonical homomorphism to the graded reduction at a point

Proposition 3.12. The tropical skeleton satisfies the following properties.
   (1) If x ∈ Sϕ (X ), then x is an Abhyankar point of X .
   (2) If U ⊂ X is a compact strictly analytic domain in X , then Sϕ (U) = Sϕ (X ) ∩ U.
   (3) The tropical skeleton Sϕ (X ) is closed in X .
   (4) If dim(T) = d = dim(X ) then Sϕ (X ) = ϕ −1 (S(T)).

Remark 3.14. Suppose that K is an algebraically closed field, that X is a smooth al-
gebraic K-curve, and that ϕalg : X ,→ T is a closed immersion. Let X = X an and ϕ =
  an
ϕalg : X an ,→ Tan , and note that ∂ X = ; by [Ber90, Theorem 3.4.1]. In this case, there is a
well-understood theory of skeletons of X associated to semistable models of the smooth
completion of X , regarded as a curve with marked points: see for instance [BPR13]. Let
S ⊂ X be such a skeleton and let τS : X → S be the canonical retraction map. By [BPR13,
Theorem 5.15], the map ϕtrop : X → NR factors through τS . It follows that ϕtrop is locally
constant on X \ S—see for example [BPR13, Lemma 2.13]—so dim ϕtrop (X , x) = 0 for
x∈ / S. Thus Theorem 3.10 yields Sϕ (X ) ⊂ S, and in fact, that Sϕ (X ) is equal to the locus
of points x ∈ S such that ϕtrop is not constant in a neighborhood of x in S. This locus can
be computed explicitly using the slope formula [BPR13, Theorem 5.15].
Example 3.15. As a specific case of Remark 3.14 taken from [BPR16, Example 2.7] (to
which the reader can refer for details), let p ≥ 5 be a prime, let K = C p , and let X = X an
for the plane curve X ⊂ G2m defined by the equation
                               x 3 y − x 2 y 2 − 2x y 3 − 3x 2 y + 2x y − p = 0.
The closure of X in the projective space P2K is given by the homogeneous equation
                            x 3 y − x 2 y 2 − 2x y 3 − 3x 2 yz + 2x yz 2 − pz 4 = 0
18                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

which defines a smooth quartic of genus 3; the zeros and poles of x and y occur at the
points P1 = (0 : 1 : 0), P2 = (1 : 0 : 0), Q 1 = (2 : 1 : 0), and Q 2 = (−1 : 1 : 0). The same
homogeneous equation describes a closed subscheme of P2K ◦ which is the minimal regular
proper semistable model Xmin of this smooth quartic. The special fiber of Xmin is given
by the equation x y(x + y − z)(x − 2 y − 2z) = 0. By [Ber90, Proposition 2.4.4], there are
unique points A, B, C, D with reduction to the special fiber equal to the generic points of
the four lines x = 0, x + y = z, y = 0 and x = 2 y +2z, respectively. The minimal skeleton
of X is given by the edges of a tetrahedron with the vertices A, B, C, D, with an additional
ray emanating from each vertex in the directions of P1 , Q 2 , P2 , and Q 1, respectively. Note
that the skeleton is the dual graph of the special fiber of Xmin with vertices corresponding
to irreducible components, edges corresponding to double points, and where the rays
correspond to the marked points P1 , P2 , Q 1, Q 2 . The tropicalization ϕtrop (X ) is a triangle
with vertices at (0, 0), (0, 1), and (1, 0), together with three rays emanating from these
vertices in the directions of (−1, −1), (3, −1), and (−1, 3), respectively. The map ϕtrop
takes A to (1, 0), C to (0, 1), and both B and D to (0, 0), and it takes each segment in
S to the line segment connecting the tropicalizations of its endpoints. Importantly, ϕtrop
takes the segment BD to the point (0, 0). See Figure 1. It follows that Sϕ (X ) consists of
S minus the interior of the segment BD.

Definition 3.16. Let S be a polyhedral set in NR of pure dimension d: in other words, S
is a finite union of d-dimensional polyhedra. Let N ′ be a free abelian group of rank d.
We say that a homomorphism Q : N → N ′ is generic with respect to S provided that for
every ω ∈ S, the image of the germ (S, ω) under Q R : NR → NR′ also has dimension d.
We say that Q : N → N ′ is generic if it is generic with respect to some unspecified S. We
apply the above terminology also to the homomorphism of tori q : T → T′ inducing the
homomorphism Q : N → N ′ on cocharacter lattices.
                 FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                              19

  The polyhedral set S is always the support of a finite polyhedral complex Π of pure
dimension d. Then Q is generic with respect to S if and only if Q R is injective on every
d-dimensional face σ ∈ Π. Note that a generic Q must have full rank,2 in which case it
embeds M ′ = Hom(M , Z) as a sublattice of M .
Theorem 3.17. Let T′ be a split torus of dimension d = dim(X ), let q : T → T′ be generic
with respect to ϕtrop (X ), and let ψ = q ◦ ϕ. Then Sϕ (X ) ∩ Int(X ) ⊂ Sψ (X ) ⊂ Sϕ (X ).
Moreover, we have Sψ (X ) = Sϕ (X ) for generic q.
Proof. Let M ′ be the character lattice of T′ . For x ∈ X we have dψ (X , x) ≤ dϕ (X , x)
because M ′ ⊂ M , so Sψ (X ) ⊂ Sϕ (X ). If x ∈ Sϕ (X ) and x ∈       / ∂ X then Theorem 3.10 yields
d = dim ϕtrop (X , x), so d = dim ψtrop (X , x) by genericity of q; hence dψ (X , x) = d and
x ∈ Sψ (X ), again by Theorem 3.10. This proves the first assertion.
    It remains to show that ∂ X ∩ Sϕ (X ) ⊂ Sψ (X ) for generic q. We induct on dim(X );
when dim(X ) = 0 there is nothing to show. By Proposition 3.12(2) we may assume
that X = M (A ) is affinoid. Let K ′ /K be a non-Archimedean extension field, define
X ′ , π, and ϕ ′ as in Proposition 3.12(5), and let ψ′ : X ′ → T′an ⊗       b K K ′ be the base change
of ψ (with respect to some choice of q). By Proposition 3.12(5), we have π(Sψ′ (X ′ )) =
Sψ (X ) and π(Sϕ′ (X ′ )) = Sϕ (X ), so if Sϕ′ (X ′ ) ⊂ Sψ′ (X ′ ) then Sϕ (X ) ⊂ Sψ (X ). Hence we
may extend scalars to assume K is algebraically closed and v(K × ) = R. In this case,
tr.deg(K  e •( M

Remark 3.19. Suppose that ϕ : X ,→ T is the inclusion of an equidimensional closed sub-
scheme. A tropical skeleton STrop (X ) was introduced in this context in [GRW17], using
Shilov points of fibers of tropicalization. We claim that Sϕ (X an ) = STrop (X ). Indeed, The-
orem 3.17 and Proposition 3.12(4) show that Sϕ (U) = Sq◦ϕ (U) = (q ◦ ϕ|U )−1 (S(T′ )) for
                FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                          21

a compact strictly analytic subdomain U of Tan and a generic homomorphism q : T → T′
to a split torus T′ of dimension d. The proof of [GRW17, Proposition 4.6] and [GRW17,
Lemma 4.4] yield STrop (X ) = (q ◦ ϕ)−1 STrop (T′ ) = (q ◦ ϕ)−1 S(T′ ). The claim follows from
Proposition 3.12(2).

   By [Duc12, Theorem 5.1], the map ϕtrop : Sϕ (X ) → ϕtrop (X ) is a piecewise (Q, Γ )-

Remark 3.21 (The d-skeleton). It follows from Remark 3.20 that the locus
                                     
                          Sϕ (X )d := x ∈ Sϕ (X ) | dim ϕtrop (X , x) = d
                                                                            p
is the union of the d-dimensional faces of Sϕ (X ). This is again a (Q, Γ )-skeleton, and
it inherits the structure of a piecwise (Z, Γ )-linear space. The map Sϕ (X )d → ϕtrop (X )
has finite fibers and surjects onto the d-dimensional part of ϕtrop (X ). We call Sϕ (X )d the
d-skeleton of (X , ϕ).
    Since dim ϕtrop (X , x) = d for any x ∈ Sϕ (X ) ∩ Int(X ) as we have seen in Theorem 3.10,
it is clear that Sϕ (X )d ∩ Int(X ) = Sϕ (X ) ∩ Int(X ).
Example 3.22. We choose X = Tan and ϕ : X → Tan the analytification of the map T → T
given by x 7→ x n for some non-zero n ∈ N. Then Sϕ (X ) = S(T), which we identify with
NR using the homeomorphism trop: S(T) → NR . Then the (Z, Γ )-linear structure of Sϕ (X )
is given by the lattice 1n N as ϕtrop : S(T) → S(T) is multiplication by n. We conclude that
                                                                                  p
ϕ induces a homeomorphism Sϕ (X ) → S(T) which is an isomorphism of (Q, Γ )-linear
spaces, but not of (Z, Γ )-linear spaces.

                                   4. TROPICAL MULTIPLICITIES
 Let X be a compact good strictly K-analytic space of pure dimension d. We fix a mo-
ment map ϕ : X → Tan to a split torus T over K, and we let N and M be the cocharacter
22                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

and character lattices of T, respectively. In this section, we will define tropical multiplici-
ties mϕ (X , x) and mϕ (X , ω) for all x ∈ X and all ω ∈ NR . For ω in the relative interior of
a d-dimensional face σ of the tropical variety ϕtrop (X ), the tropical multiplicity mϕ (X , ω)
agrees with the tropical weight of σ induced by the construction of Chambert-Loir and
Ducros (see Remark 4.10), so if ϕ is the analytification of the embedding of a closed sub-
variety then we get the classical tropical weight of σ. In this algebraic setting, we will
show in Lemma 4.15 that mϕ (X , x) agrees with the relative tropical multiplicity mrel (x)
introduced in [BPR16] for all x ∈ X , and that mϕ (X , ω) agrees with the tropical multi-
plicity mTrop (ω) defined in ibid. for all ω ∈ NR . As our tropical multiplicities are defined
for all points of X and NR , including boundary points, they provide a common gener-
alization of these constructions. Our definition also has the advantage that it does not
depend a priori on any choices.
   Similarly as in the previous section, all results extend to Γ -strictly analytic spaces over
K for any subgroup Γ of R containing the value group of K.
 If A is an Artin local ring, its length is defined to be the length of A as a module over A.
We will use the tropical skeleton Sϕ (X ) and the notation from Section 3.

with respect to ϕ is defined to be

where e(L/K) is the ramification index and f (L/K) is the classical residue degree. Recall
that a non-archimedean field K is called stable if e(F /K) f (F /K) = [F : K] for any finite
field extension F /K (the inequality ≤ holds in general by [BGR84, Proposition 3.1.3/2]).
If K is a stable field, then e(L/K) f (L/K) = [L : K], so m′ϕ (X , x) = mϕ (X , x), and it is not
necessary to pass to the completion of an algebraic closure in this case.

   We take the following example of a non-archimedean field K which is not stable from
[BGR84]. Let K ′ be the completion of an algebraic closure of Q2 and let K be the topo-
logical closure of the subfield of K ′ generated bySall 2n -roots of 2. Then K is a non-
archimedean field which has additive value group n 21n Z. It follows that every quadratic
field extension of Kpis unramified over K. It is shown in the example given in [BGR84,
§3.6.1] that L = K( 3) satisfies f (L/K) = 1, so that K is not stable. For this choice of L,
we have m′ϕ (X , x) = e(L/K) f (L/K) = 1, but mϕ (X , ω) = [L : K] = 2.
4.4 (Fundamental Cycles). In [Gub98, 2.7], the fundamental cycle [X ] of the analytic
space X was defined as follows. Let U = M (A ) be a strictly affinoidP domain in X . The
cycle [Spec(A )] of the noetherian scheme Spec(A ) has the form Y ′ mY ′ [Y ′ ], where the
sum runs over the irreducible components Y ′ of Spec(A ). This construction is compatible
with pull-back with respect to flat morphisms of noetherian schemes, from which P   it fol-
lows that these cycles glue to give a cycle [X ]. This cycle has the form [X ] = Y mY [Y ],
the sum being taken over all irreducible components Y of X (see [Duc18, 1.5] for a def-
inition). In particular, there is a canonical multiplicity mY associated to an irreducible
component Y .
   The next lemma relates multiplicities of irreducible components to tropical multiplic-
ities. As a special case, if K is algebraically closed then the factor of length(OX ,x ) in
Definition 4.1 is simply the multiplicity of the irreducible component of X containing x.
Lemma 4.5. Let ι : X red ,→ X be the reduction of X and let ψ = ϕ ◦ ι. If x ∈ Sϕ (X ), then x
is contained in a unique irreducible component Y of X , and mϕ (X , x) = mY mψ (X red , x).
Proof. Since multiplicities are local, we may pass to a neighbourhood of x to assume
that X = M (A ) is strictly affinoid. If x ∈ Sϕ (X ) then x is an Abhyankar point by
Proposition 3.12(1), so it is contained in a unique irreducible component Y by [Duc18,
Remark 1.5.9]. Let p ⊂ A be the kernel of the semi-norm corresponding to x. Then
mY = length(Ap ). The homomorphism Ap → OX ,x is flat by [Ber93, Theorem 2.1.4], and
pOX ,x is the maximal ideal of OX ,x by [Duc18, Example 3.2.10], so mY = length(OX ,x )
by [Ful98, Lemma A.4.1].
   Let K ′ be the completion of an algebraic closure of K, let X ′ = X ⊗

                                  ′         ′   ′
                                        bKK : X → T ⊗ an      ′

π (x) ⊂ Sϕ′ (X ) by Proposition 3.12(6). For x ∈ π (x) the map OX ,x → OX ′ ,x ′ is flat
by [Ber93, Corollary 2.1.3]. Again it follows from that [Ful98, Lemma A.4.1] that
     length(OX ′ ,x ′ ) = length(OX ,x ) length(OX ′ ,x ′ /mX ,x OX ′ ,x ′ ) = mY length(OX ′ ,x ′ /mX ,x OX ′ ,x ′ ).

Remark 4.10. Recall from 3.2 that we can choose a (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex Π with
support ϕtrop (X ) such that ϕtrop (∂ X ) is contained in a subcomplex of dimension at most

only if ϕtrop (X , x) is d-dimensional, which means here that it is the germ of σ at ω. If
this is the case, then for ψ = q ◦ ϕ, we see that ψtrop (X , x) is the germ of NR′ at Q R (ω),

relint(σ) to define a canonical calibration on σ. This is equivalent to defining the trop-
ical multiplicity mσ of σ in ϕtrop (X ) by the right hand side of (4.10.1). See [Gub16,
Remark 7.6].
  For convenience of the reader, we repeat here this crucial argument of Chambert-Loir
and Ducros in the context of our tropical multiplicities.

By [CD12, 2.4.3], the map ψ is finite and flat over a neighbourhood of ξ′ . It follows from

Definition 4.12. Choose a (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex Π with support ϕtrop (X ) such that
ϕtrop (∂ X ) is contained in a subcomplex of dimension at most d − 1, as in Remark 4.10.
Let σ be a d-dimensional face of Π. Then we define the tropical weight of σ by mσ :=
mϕ (X , σ) := mϕ (X , ω) for any ω ∈ relint(σ). This is well-defined by Proposition 4.11.
Remark 4.13. We have seen in Remark 3.20 that the tropical skeleton Sϕ (X ) has the
structure of a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear space such that ϕtrop induces a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear
map to ϕtrop (X ) which is finite to one. Replacing Π from Definition 4.12 by a suitable
subdivision, this piecewise linear structure can be given by an abstract (Z, Γ )-polytopal
complex with support Sϕ (X ) such that ϕtrop maps every face ∆ of this complex by a (Z, Γ )-
linear isomorphism onto a face σ of Π. If dim(∆) = d, then we claim that mϕ (X , x) is
constant in x ∈ relint(∆) and we define the tropical weight m∆ := mϕ (X , ∆) := mϕ (X , x)
in ∆. This makes Sϕ (X ) into a weighted abstract (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex supported on
the d-skeleton Sϕ (X )d (Remark 3.21).
   To show this, we note first that we may shrink X to assume that Sϕ (X ) = ∆ by using
Remark 4.2(1) and Proposition 3.12(2). Then for x ∈ relint(∆), we have ω := ϕtrop (x) ∈
relint(σ) and mϕ (X , x) = mϕ (X , ω). Hence the claim follows from Proposition 4.11.

                              5. PIECEWISE LINEAR FUNCTIONS
   In this section we discuss piecewise linear functions on analytic spaces. Most of these
results are standard in one form or another: for instance, see [CD12, Gub98, GM19].
However, the terminology in the literature is somewhat nonstandard, so it is important
to fix our ideas. We will find it useful to work slightly more generally with piecewise
linear functions with slopes contained in an additive subgroup Λ ⊂ R, and in some cases
new arguments are needed. In practice we will usually take Λ = Z, Q, or R, in which case
either Λ = Z or Λ is divisible.
   In this section, K is a non-Archimedean field with a non-trivial valuation v and value
group Γ := v(K × ) ⊂ R. We consider a good strictly K-analytic space X and an additive
subgroup Λ ⊂ R. Recall from 2.3 that a strictly analytic space is good provided that that
every point has a strictly affinoid neighbourhood. Most strictly analytic spaces occurring
in practice are good: for example, any strictly affinoid space, a space with no boundary,
or an analytic subdomain of the analytification of an algebraic variety. We restrict our
attention to good spaces as then the coherent modules on the Berkovich space X are
the same as the coherent modules on the space X G endowed with the G-topology [Ber93,
Proposition 1.3.4], which makes it reasonable to work with line bundles on X . We always
               FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                       31

use the G-topology on X generated by the strictly affinoid domains. Let C(X ) be the space
of continuous real functions on X .
Definition 5.1. A function h: X → R is called piecewise Λ-linear or Λ-PL if there exists a
                                                                                                    ×
G-covering {XP i } of X such that for each i, there are finitely many λi j ∈ Λ and f i j ∈ O (X i )
with h|X i = j λi j log | f i j |. A Z-PL function is called piecewise linear or PL.
  We denote the group of PL functions on X by PL(X ), and the group of Λ-PL functions
by PLΛ (X ). These are subgroups of C(X ).
5.2. Some remarks:
   (1) A function h: X → R is PL if and only if there exists a G-covering {X i } of X and
       f i ∈ O (X i )× such that h|X i = log | f i |.
   (2) The set of Λ-PL functions on X forms a group since any two G-coverings {X i } and
       {X ′j } admit the common refinement {X i ∩ X ′j }.
   (3) The restriction of a Λ-PL function to a strictly analytic domain is Λ-PL.
   (4) The presheaf U 7→ PLΛ (U) is a sheaf in the G-topology, and therefore in the ana-
       lytic topology as well.
   (5) A function h: X → R is Λ-PL if and only if there exists a G-covering {X i } of
       X suchPthat for each i, there are finitely many λi j ∈ Λ and hi j ∈ PL(X i ) with
       h|X i = j λi j hi j .
   (6) A function h: X → R is Q-PL if and only if, locally in the analytic topology, there
       exists a positive integer N such that N h is PL.
   (7) If Λ = Z or Λ = Q then the minimum and maximum of two Λ-PL functions is
       again Λ-PL by [GM19, Proposition 2.12(d)].
Example 5.3. The assertion of 5.2(7) is not true for Λ strictly containing Q. For example,
if the value group of K is Q and X = Gan m
                                            = Spec(K[t ±1])an is the split torus of rank 1, then
the tropicalization of any strictly affinoid domain in X is a union of compact intervals with
rational vertices. We conclude that for Λ = R, the function h := π log |t|+1 = −π trop +1
is Λ-PL, but max(h, 0) is not Λ-PL as max(h, 0) = ϕ ◦ trop for ϕ(u) = max(−πu + 1, 0),
and as the piecewise linear function ϕ has a bend at the irrational number 1/π.
  Here we list some basic functoriality properties of Λ-PL functions.
Lemma 5.4. Let h: X → R be a function.
   (1) Let f : X ′ → X be a morphism of good strictly K-analytic spaces. If h ∈ PLΛ (X ) then
       h ◦ f ∈ PLΛ (X ′ ).
   (2) Let K ′ /K be a non-Archimedean field extension, let X ′ = X ⊗
                                                                    b K K ′ , and let π: X ′ → X
       be the structure morphism. If h ∈ PLΛ (X ) then h ◦ π ∈ PLΛ (X ′ ).
   (3) Let ι : X red → X be the inclusion of the reduction of X , and let h′ = h ◦ ι. Then
       h ∈ PLΛ (X ) if and only if h′ ∈ PLΛ (X red ).
Proof. The first two assertions are obvious. For the third, by (1), the only thing to show is
that if h′ is Λ-PL then h is Λ-PL. Let U = M (A ) ⊂ X be a strictly    Paffinoid domain, and let
                                                           ′                                    ×
Ured = M (Ared ) ⊂ X red be its reduction. Suppose that P h |Ured = λi log |g i | for g i ∈ Ared  .
If f i ∈ A lifts g i then f i ∈ A × is a unit, and h|U = λi log | f i |. Since X red has a G-cover
by such affinoid domains, the same is true for X , so h is Λ-PL.                                 
32                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

   At this point we assume that the reader is familiar with formal geometry as recalled
in 2.4. We give an interpretation of Λ-PL functions in terms of formal geometry which
we will use to prove some more subtle properties of piecewise Λ-linearity. Recall that
a formal K ◦ -model of a paracompact strictly K-analytic space X is an admissible formal
scheme X over K ◦ equipped with an identification Xη = X .

     A frame of a line bundle on an open set is a nowhere vanishing section.

5.5 (Functions from Formal Models of OX ). We assume now that X is paracompact. This
allows us to use Raynaud’s theory of formal K ◦ -models, as in 2.4. We will later apply
this only locally on a good, strictly analytic space, where we can always find a compact
strictly analytic neighbourhood (even a strictly affinoid neighbourhood as X is good).
Hence the reader loses nothing by assuming here and in the following that X is compact.
(Paracompactness has the advantage that one can apply these considerations also to the
analytification of algebraic varieties over K.)
   Let X be a formal K ◦ -model of X . The isomorphism classes of line bundles L on X
equipped with an identification Lη = OX form an abelian group denoted by M (X). Then
L ∈ M (X) determines a canonical metric k kL on OX , defined as follows: if s is a frame of
L over a formal open subset U ⊂ X, then we set ksk = 1 on Uη . This metric is well-defined
because any two frames differ by a unit with constant absolute value 1.
   Equivalently, we get a canonical function hL = − log k1kL : X → R with − log k f kL =
− log | f |+hL for an analytic function f on any open subset U of X . If U = Uη for a formal
open subset U of X and if f ∈ Γ (U, L) ⊂ Γ (Uη , OX ) is a frame of L, then by definition
0 = − log | f | + hL , so hL is PL because X has a G-cover by such U = Uη .

5.6 (PL Functions are Model Functions). If X is paracompact, then [GM19, Proposi-
tion 2.10] yields that h: X → R is PL if and only if there exists a formal K ◦ -model (X, L)
of (X , OX ) such that h = − log k1kL .

5.7 (Λ-PL Functions from Formal Models). For paracompact X , we extend 5.6 to the
case of Λ-PL functions as follows. Choose a formal K ◦ -model X of X , and set M (X)Λ :=
M (X)⊗Z Λ. We have a unique homomorphism h: M (X)Λ −→ C(X ), written        Pm L 7→ hL , such
that hλL =P   −λ log k1kL for all L ∈ M (X) and λ ∈ Λ. Note that if L = i=1 λi Li ∈ M (X)Λ
                m
then hL = i=1 λi hLi ∈ PLΛ (X ) by 5.2(5).
   If f : X′ → X is a morphism of formal K ◦ -models of X , then h f ∗ L = hL for all L ∈ M (X)Λ .
More generally, if f : X′ → X is any morphism of separated admissible formal schemes
then h f ∗ L = hL ◦ fη for all L ∈ M (X)Λ . (See 2.4 for separatedness of formal models.)
   For L ∈ M (X)Λ , we get a unique continuous metric k kL on OX defined by − log k f kL =
− log | f | + hL for an analytic function f on any open subset U of X .

   We want to extend the result of 5.6 locally in the analytic topology to Λ-coefficients as
in 5.7. To do this we need to show that a Λ-PL function is locally in the analytic topology a
linear combination of PL functions with Λ-coefficients. (For our purposes, we could have
taken the statement of Proposition 5.8 as the definition of Λ-PL; we chose Definition 5.1
because it is more compatible with the terminology in the literature.)

  With these foundations in place, we can use formal models to show that piecewise
Λ-linearity can sometimes be checked after pull-back, at least when Λ is divisible.
Proposition 5.11. Let f : X ′ → X be a finite, flat, surjective morphism of good strictly K-
analytic spaces. Suppose that Λ is divisible. If h: X → R is a function and h′ = h ◦ f , then
h ∈ PLΛ (X ) if and only if h′ ∈ PLΛ (X ′ ).
Proof. By Lemma 5.4(1), we only need to show that if h′ is Λ-PL then so is h. By [Ber93,
Proposition 3.2.7], the map f is open. Let x ∈ X , choosePx ′ ∈ X ′ mapping to x, and

let U ′ be an open neighbourhood of x ′ such that h′ |U ′ = i=1 λi h′i for λ1 , . . . , λm ∈ Λ
and h′1 , . . . , h′m ∈ PL(U ′ ). Such a neighbourhood exists by Proposition 5.8. By [Ber93,
Lemma 3.1.2, Definition 3.2.5], after shrinking U ′ , the morphism f |U ′ : U ′ →Pf (U ′ ) is
                                                                                        m
finite and flat. Thus we may replace X ′ by U ′ and X by f (U ′ ) to assume h′ = i=1 λi h′i
on X ′ . Shrinking further, we may assume X and X ′ are compact. By 5.6, each PL function
h′i is induced by a line bundle on a formal model of X ′ . Passing to a dominating formal

model, we may assume that there is a formal K ◦ -model X′ of X ′ and L′i ∈ M (X′ ) such
that h′i = hL′i for each i.
   Using Raynaud’s theorem [Bos14, Theorem 8.4.3, Lemma 8.4.4], after passing to an
admissible formal blowup, we can extend f to a morphism f : X′ → X for some for-
mal K ◦ -model X of X . By [BL93b, Theorem 5.2, Corollary 5.3(b)], after passing to
admissible formal blowups of X and X′ , we may assume f : X′ → X is flat and quasi-
finite. By [Tem00, Corollary 4.4] the morphism f : X′s → Xs is proper, so it is finite; by
Nakayama’s lemma, the morphism f : X′ → X is finite. Working locally, we may thus
assume X = Spf(A) and X′ = Spf(A′ ) are formal affines, with X connected. Then A′ is a
finite, flat A-module, which is locally of finite presentation by [Bos14, Theorem 7.3.4],
so it is a locally free A-module of some constant rank n by [Sta21, Lemma 02KB]. Thus
we can define the norm N (L′ ) of a line bundle L′ on X′ as in [EGAII, 6.5]. This is a line
bundle on X and a model of OX⊗n = OX , so we get a homomorphism N : M (X′ ) → M (X).
Tensoring with Λ, we have an induced homomorphism N : M (X′ )Λ → M (X)Λ .
   Now we can transfer the considerations in [BE21, 8.5] to the analytic setting. For

     Proposition 5.11 gives the following variant of Lemma 5.4(2).
Corollary 5.12. Let X be a good strictly K-analytic space, let K ′ be the completion of an
algebraic field extension of K, let X ′ = X ⊗
                                            b K K ′ , and let π: X ′ → X be the structure morphism.
Suppose that Λ is divisible. If h: X → R is a function and h′ = h ◦ π, then h ∈ PLΛ (X ) if and
only if h′ ∈ PLΛ (X ′ ).
Proof. By Lemma 5.4(2), we only need to show that if h′ is Λ-PL then so is h. When K ′
is a finite extension of K then this is a special case of Proposition 5.11. We reduce to this
case as follows.
    Since piecewise Λ-linearity is a local property in the analytic topology, we may assume
that X is compact. Then X ′ is also compact, so by Proposition 5.8 there are finitely many

to X ′ agree with hi j . Then the pull-back of the Λ-PL function hi := j λi j hi j agrees with
h′i on X ′ . As seen at the beginning, it is enough to show that the pull-back of h to X F is

we show it in a neighbourhood of any y ∈ X . It follows from [Ber90, Proposition 1.3.5,
Corollary 1.3.6] that the structure map p : X ′ → X is open and surjective, and hence the
sets p(Vi′ ) form an open covering of X . For y ∈ X , we choose i such that y ∈ p(Vi′ ). For
all x ′ ∈ Vi′ and x = p(x ′ ), we have h(x) = h′ (x ′ ) = h′i (x ′ ) = hi (x), proving that h is Λ-PL
on the neighbourhood p(Vi′ ) of y.                                                                 
