                 6. REDUCTION OF GERMS AND RESIDUES OF PL FUNCTIONS
   We will characterize semipositive and harmonic functions in 7 in the language of
Temkin’s theory of reductions of germs [Tem00], using residues of line bundles with
piecewise linear metrics from [CD12, Section 6]. In this section, we recall the theory
following quite closely [CD12, 6.1], and prove then the preliminary results we need.
                  FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                                   37

  By a variety over a field k we mean an integral, separated k-scheme V of finite type.
The field of rational functions on V is denoted R(V ).

6.1 (Riemann–Zariski spaces). Let k be a field, let F /k be a field extension, and let PF /k
be the set of valuations on F that are trivial on k, up to equivalence. For ν ∈ PF /k we let
Oν ⊂ F denote the corresponding valuation ring. For a subset A ⊂ F we set PF /k {A} = {ν ∈
PF /k | ν( f ) ≥ 0 for all f ∈ A}. Subsets of the form PF /k { f1 , . . . , f n } are said to be affine.
Note that PF /k = PF /k {;} is itself affine. We endow PF /k with the topology generated by
affine subsets. We equip PF /k with the sheaf of rings O : U 7→ { f | ν( f ) ≥ 0 for all ν ∈ U}.
For ν ∈ PF /k the localization of O at ν is canonically identified with Oν , so PF /k is a
locally ringed space. It is shown in [Tem00, Corollary 1.3] (see [Tem04, Section 2] for
an erratum) that any affine open subset of PF /k is quasi-compact.

6.2 (Models). We keep the notation in 6.1. Let V be a variety equipped with a k-algebra
homomorphism from the function field R(V ) to F . We let PF /k {V } denote the set of all val-
uations ν ∈ PF /k such that Spec(F ) → V extends to a morphism Spec(Oν ) → V . Such an
extension is unique if it exists, so there is a map of sets πV : PF /k {V } → V sending ν to the
image of the closed point of Spec(Oν ) → V . It is easy to see that PF /k {V } is a finite union
of affine open subsets, so it is quasi-compact. The morphism πV : PF /k {V } → V is a surjec-
tive morphism of ringed spaces by [CD12, 6.1.1]. Conversely, if U is any quasi-compact
open subset of PF /k then U = PF /k {V } for some such V by [Tem00, Proposition 1.4]; we
call V a model of U.3
   Let V and V ′ be varieties equipped with k-algebra homomorphisms R(V ) → F and
R(V ′ ) → F . A dominant morphism V ′ → V , compatible with the morphisms R(V ) → F
and R(V ′ ) → F , is unique if it exists, in which case PF /k {V ′ } ⊂ PF /k {V }. In this case
we say that V ′ dominates V . We have equality PF /k {V ′ } = PF /k {V } if and only if V ′ →
V is proper by the valuative criterion of properness for integral schemes (see [EGAII,
Corollaire 7.3.10(ii)] and [Tem11, Lemma 3.2.1]).

6.3 (Functoriality). Let F ′ /k′ be an extension of F /k: that is, F ′ is an extension field
of F and k′ is a subfield of F ′ containing k. Restriction of valuations gives a canonical
map ϕ : PF ′ /k′ → PF /k . The inverse image of PF /k { f1 , . . . , f n } is PF ′ /k′ { f1 , . . . , f n }, so ϕ is
continuous. One checks that ϕ induces a morphism of locally ringed spaces. If k′ = k
then ϕ is surjective, and if F ′ = F then ϕ is injective.
   Let V (resp. V ′ ) be a k-variety (resp. k′ -variety) equipped with a k-algebra homomor-
phism R(V ) → F (resp. a k′ -algebra homomorphism R(V ′ ) → F ′ ). As in 6.2, a domi-
nant morphism V ′ → V of k-schemes, compatible with the morphisms R(V ) → F and
R(V ′ ) → F ′ , is unique if it exists, in which case ϕ(P F ′ /k′ {V ′ }) ⊂ P F /k {V }. In this case we
also say that V ′ dominates V .

Lemma 6.4. Let F ′ /k′ be an extension of F /k, and let ϕ : PF ′ /k′ → PF /k be the canonical
morphism. Let V be a k-variety equipped with a k-algebra homomorphism R(V ) → F , and
let V ′ be the closure of the image of the canonical k′ -morphism Spec(F ′ ) → V ⊗k k′ . Then
PF ′ /k′ {V ′ } = ϕ −1 (PF /k {V }).
   3
    In [CD12] these are called premodels.
38                             W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

Proof. This is similar to [Tem11, Lemma 3.2.1]. Let ν′ ∈ PF ′ /k′ and let ν = ϕ(ν′ ) = ν′ | F .
If ν′ ∈ PF ′ /k′ {V ′ } then we have a k′ -morphism Spec(Oν′ ) → V ′ extending Spec(F ′ ) →
V ′ . Let x ∈ V be the image of the closed point of Spec(Oν′ ) under the composition
Spec(Oν′ ) → V ′ → V . Then Spec(Oν′ ) → V factors through Spec(OV,x ), so we have a
k-algebra homomorphism OV,x → Oν′ . It follows that the image of OV,x is contained in the
intersection Oν = Oν′ ∩ F , so Spec(F ) → V extends to a morphism Spec(Oν ) → V , and
hence ν ∈ PF /k {V }. Conversely, suppose that ν ∈ PF /k {V }. Then Spec(Oν′ ) maps to V via
Spec(Oν′ ) → Spec(Oν ) → V , so Spec(F ′ ) → V ⊗k k′ extends to Spec(Oν′ ) → V ⊗k k′ . The
image of this morphism is contained in V ′ , so ν′ ∈ PF ′ /k′ {V ′ }.                      
  As a special case of the next lemma, we see that the poset of models of a quasi-compact
open subset U ⊂ PF /k is filtered.
Lemma 6.5. Let F ′ /k′ be an extension of F /k, and let ϕ : PF ′ /k′ → PF /k be the canonical
morphism. Let U ′ ⊂ PF ′ /k′ and U ⊂ PF /k be quasi-compact open subsets such that ϕ(U ′ ) ⊂ U.
Let V ′ and V be models of U ′ and U, respectively. Then there is a model W ′ of U ′ that
dominates both V and V ′ .
Proof. Let W be the closure of the image of the canonical k′ -morphism Spec(F ′ ) → V ⊗k k′ ,
as in Lemma 6.4. Then this lemma shows that W → V is dominant and ϕ −1 (U) =
PF ′ /k′ {W }, so we can replace V by W to assume k = k′ and F = F ′ . The given morphisms
Spec(F ) → V and Spec(F ) → V ′ determine a morphism Spec(F ) → V ×k V ′ ; let W ′ be the
closure of the image of this morphism. This is a variety. The generic point of W ′ maps to
the generic points of V and V ′ , so W ′ → V and W ′ → V ′ are dominant. We must show that
PF /k {W ′ } = U. For ν ∈ U we have unique morphisms Spec(O v ) → V and Spec(O v ) → V ′ ,
which induces a morphism p : Spec(O v ) → V ×k V ′ extending Spec(F ) → V ×k V ′ . The
image of p is contained in W ′ , so ν ∈ PF /k {W ′ }.                                     
6.6 (Invertible sheaves on Riemann–Zariski spaces). With the notation in 6.1, let U ⊂
PF /k be a quasi-compact open subset. Then U is a ringed space, so it makes sense to
discuss invertible sheaves on U. We let Pic(U) denote the group of isomorphism classes
of invertible sheaves, and for an additive subgroup Λ ⊂ R we set Pic(U)Λ = Pic(U) ⊗Z Λ.
   Let V be a model of U as in 6.2, with canonical morphism πV : U → V . If L V is an
invertible sheaf on V , then L := π∗V L V is an invertible sheaf on U. It is an important fact
that every invertible sheaf on U arises in this way. This is explained in [CD12, 6.1.3]. We
call (V, L V ) a model of (U, L).
   More generally, for an additive subgroup Λ of R we have a group homomorphism
Pn )Λ → Pic(U)Λ . Any element of Pic(U)Λ can be expressed as a finite sum L =
Pic(V
      λ L for λi ∈ Λ and L i ∈ Pic(U). Choosing a model for each L i and passing to a
   i=1 i i
dominating model, we see that any L ∈ Pic(U)Λ has the form π∗V L V for a model V of U
and L V ∈ Pic(V )Λ . We call such (V, L V ) a model of (U, L) as above.
6.7 (Nef line bundles). Let X be a separated k-scheme of finite type and let L be a line
bundle on X . For an integral, proper closed curve C ⊂ X we have a well-defined inter-
section number c1 (L).C := deg(L|C ) ∈ Z. We say that L is numerically effective or nef if
c1 (L).C ≥ 0 for all such curves C, and that L is numerically trivial if c1 (L).C = 0 for all C
(equivalently, if L and L −1 are both nef).
               FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                     39

   More generally, let Λ ⊂ R be an additive subgroup and let C ⊂ X be an integral, proper
closed curve. We have an induced homomorphism L 7→ c1 (L).C : Pic(X )Λ → Λ, and we
say that L is nef (resp. numerically trivial) if c1 (L).C ≥ 0 (resp. c1 (L).C = 0) for all such C.
   The set of nef line bundles in Pic(X )Λ forms a monoid (resp. a cone if Λ = R), and the
set of numerically trivial line bundles form a subgroup (resp. an R-subspace).
  Here we list some classical properties of nefness. We sketch some proofs because in
the literature, the scheme X is generally assumed to be proper.
Lemma 6.8. Let X be a separated k-scheme of finite type and let L ∈ Pic(X )Λ .
    (1) The line bundle L is nef if and only if its image in Pic(X )R is nef.
    (2) Let k′ /k be a field extension and let X ′ = X ⊗k k′ , with structure morphism π: X ′ →
        X . Then L is nef if and only if π∗ L is nef.
    (3) Let f : X ′ → X be a morphism of separated, finite-type k-schemes. If L is nef then
        f ∗ L is nef, and the converse holds if f is proper and surjective.
    (4) Let ι : X red → X be the reduction map. Then L is nef if and only if ι ∗ L is nef.
The same statements hold with “nef” replaced by “numerically trivial.”
Proof. The last assertion holds because L is numerically trivial if and only if L and L −1
are nef. Assertion (1) is obvious because nefness is a numerical property, and (4) is clear
because X and X red have the same integral subcurves.
   (2) Since intersection numbers are compatible with base extension, it is clear that
if π∗ L is nef, then also L is nef. To prove the converse, we assume that L is nef. First
suppose that k′ /k is algebraic. Since every integral proper subcurve C ′ of X ′ is defined
over a finite subextension, we may assume that k′ /k is a finite extension. Then we may
view X ′ as a scheme of finite type over k and π as a finite morphism over k. Then π(C ′ )
is a proper subcurve of X and we get c1 (π∗ L).C ′ = c1 (L).π∗ (C ′ ) ≥ 0 by the projection
formula. This proves the algebraic case of (2), so we may assume k is algebraically
closed to prove the claim in general. Let C ′ again be an integral proper subcurve of X ′ .
Since C ′ is defined over a finitely generated subextension, we may assume that k′ is a
finitely generated field extension of k. Then k′ is the function field of a variety V over k.
Let πV : X ×k V → V and πX : X ×k V → X be the structure morphisms. The morphism
π: X ′ → X is the generic fiber of πV . Since C ′ is projective and flat over k′ , the spreading
out property shows that there is a non-empty open subset U of V such that the closure C ′
of C ′ in π−1
            V
              (U) is flat and projective over U. By [Kol96, Proposition VI.2.9], the degree
of the fiber Cu′ with respect to π∗X (L) is independent of the choice of u ∈ U. Let η be the
generic point of U and let u be a closed point of U. Since Cu′ ∼      = C for a proper closed
subcurve C of X , the claim follows from
            c1 (π∗ L).C ′ = degπ∗V (L) (Cη′ ) = degπ∗V (L) (Cu′ ) = deg L (C) = c1 (L).C ≥ 0.

   (3) If L is nef then f ∗ L is nef because if C ′ ⊂ X ′ is an integral proper k-curve then
f (C ′ ) ⊂ X is integral and proper of dimension ≤ 1, so the claim follows from the projec-
tion formula. Conversely, if f ∗ L is nef and if f is a proper surjective map, then L is nef
because every integral proper k-curve C ⊂ X is the image of an integral proper k-curve
C ′ ⊂ X ′ (take the closure of a closed point in the fiber over the generic point of C). 
40                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

Definition 6.9. Let U be a quasi-compact open subset of PF /k , let Λ ⊂ R be an additive
subgroup, and let L ∈ Pic(U)Λ . We say that L is nef if there exists a model (V, L V ) of
(U, L) with L V nef. We say that L is numerically trivial if both L and L −1 are nef.
6.10. The set of nef elements of Pic(U)Λ forms a monoid (pass to a dominating model
and use Lemma 6.8(3)), resp. a cone if Λ = R; the set of numerically trivial elements
forms a group (resp. an R-subspace). An element of Pic(U)Λ is nef if and only if its image
in Pic(U)R is nef.
     The following lemma shows that nefness can be checked on any model.
Lemma 6.11. Let U be a quasi-compact open subset of PF /k and let L ∈ Pic(U)Λ be nef. If
(V, L V ) is a model of L, then L V is nef.
Proof. By definition, there is a model (V ′ , L V ′ ) of L such that L V ′ is nef. Let W be a model
dominating both V and V ′ as in Lemma 6.5, and let LW and LW         ′
                                                                        be the pullbacks of L V and
L V ′ to W , respectively. It is shown in [CD12, 6.1.3] that Pic(U) is the inductive limit of
the groups Pic(V ) over all models V of U. Since tensor products commute with inductive
limits, we have Pic(U)Λ = lim Pic(V )Λ . Hence we can replace W by a larger model such
               ′
                               −→
that LW = LW     in Pic(W ). Then LW is nef, so L V ′ is nef because W → V ′ is proper, using
Lemma 6.8(3) twice.                                                                               
     The next lemma is an analogue of Lemma 6.8.
Lemma 6.12. Let F ′ /k′ be an extension of F /k, and let ϕ : PF ′ /k′ → PF /k be the canonical
morphism. Let U ′ ⊂ PF ′ /k′ and U ⊂ PF /k be quasi-compact open subsets such that ϕ(U ′ ) ⊂ U.
If L ∈ Pic(U)Λ is nef, then ϕ ∗ L ∈ Pic(U ′ )Λ is nef. The converse is true if U ′ = ϕ −1(U), and
if one of the following conditions holds:
    (1) k′ /k is algebraic, or
    (2) tr.deg(F /k) < ∞ and tr.deg(k′ F /k′ ) = tr.deg(F /k).
The same statements hold with “nef” replaced by “numerically trivial.”
  Here k′ F denotes the smallest subfield of F ′ containing both k′ and F . It is always true
that tr.deg(k′ F /k′ ) ≤ tr.deg(F /k).
Proof. Suppose that L is nef. Let (V, L V ) be a model of (U, L), and let W be the closure
of the image of the canonical k′ -morphism Spec(F ′ ) → V ⊗k k′ , as in Lemma 6.4. The
pullback of L to W is nef by Lemma 6.8(2,3), so we may replace V by W to assume
k = k′ . By Lemma 6.5, there is a model V ′ of U ′ dominating V ; let f : V ′ → V be the
dominating morphism. Then f ∗ L V is nef by Lemma 6.8(3), so ϕ ∗ L is nef.
   Now suppose that ϕ ∗ L is nef and that U ′ = ϕ −1 (U). First we treat the case when k′ is
algebraic over k. If k′ = k then any model (V, L V ) of (U, L) is also model of (U ′ , ϕ ∗ L), so
L V is nef by Lemma 6.11. In general, the map PF ′ /k′ → PF /k factors as PF ′ /k′ → PF ′ /k →
PF /k , so by the previous sentence we may assume F = F ′ . Note that PF /k = PF /k′ and
U = U ′ . Let (V, L V ) be a model of L and let V ′ be a model of U ′ . We may assume that V ′
dominates V by Lemma 6.5; let f : V ′ → V be the dominating morphism. Then (V ′ , f ∗ L V )
is a model of (U ′ , ϕ ∗ L), so f ∗ L V is nef. The model V ′ is defined over a finite extension of
k: that is, there exists a finite extension k′′ /k contained in k′ and a variety V ′′ over k′′
                FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                           41

such that V ′ = V ′′ ⊗k′′ k′ . After enlarging k′′ , we may assume that V ′′ also dominates V ;
let f ′ : V ′′ → V be the dominating morphism. By Lemma 6.8(2), the line bundle f ′∗ L is
nef. Replacing k′ with k′′ and V ′ with V ′′ , we may assume that [k′ : k] < ∞. In this case,
the variety V ′ is also a model of U. Any proper k-curve in V ′ is also a proper k′ -curve, so
f ∗ L V is nef when we consider V ′ as a k-variety. It follows from Lemma 6.11 that L is nef.
   Now assume that d = tr.deg(F /k) < ∞, and that tr.deg(k′ F /k′ ) = d. First we reduce
to the case when k is algebraically closed. Let F ′′ be a field containing F ′ and an algebraic
closure k of k. Pulling back via PF ′′ /k′ → PF ′ /k′ , we may replace F ′ with F ′′ to assume
k ⊂ F ′ . We have tr.deg(kF /k) = tr.deg(F /k) = d, and

                  tr.deg(k′ kF /k′ k) = tr.deg(k′ kF /k′ ) = tr.deg(F k′ /k′ ) = d.

Applying case (1) to the extension kF /k of F /k and using the above displayed equation,
we may replace k by k, F by kF , and k′ by k′ k to assume k = k.
   Choose a model (V, L V ) of (U, L) with dim(V ) = d. Since k is algebraically closed, the
scheme V ′ := V ⊗k k′ is a variety. As F /R(V ) is algebraic, we have tr.deg(k′ R(V )/k′ ) =
tr.deg(k′ F /k′ ) = d. Let x be the image of Spec(F ′ ) → V ′ , so x maps to the generic point
of V . The residue field κ(x) contains both k′ and R(V ), so tr.deg(κ(x)/k′ ) = d. It follows
that x is the generic point of V ′ . By Lemma 6.4, the variety V ′ is a model of U ′ , so ϕ ∗ L V
is nef by Lemma 6.11, hence L V is nef by Lemma 6.8(3).                                        
6.13 (Temkin’s reductions of germs). Let X be a good strictly K-analytic space and let
x ∈ X . Temkin’s reduction red(X , x) of the germ (X , x) is an affine open subset of
PHf(x)/ek , where H    f(x) is the reduction of the completed residue field H (x) at x as in 2.3.

is a canonical homomorphism R(VU,x ) → H             f(x) by [Ber90, 2.4]. We define red(X , x)

Lemma 2.2]. Let πU,x : red(X , x) → VU,x be the canonical morphism of 6.2
   Reduction of germs (X , x) 7→ red(X , x) is functorial in (X , x). It also respects extension

     We will also use the following theorem of Temkin.
Theorem 6.15 ([Tem00, Theorem 4.1]). Let f : X ′ → X be a morphism of good strictly
K-analytic spaces, let x ′ ∈ X ′ , and let x = f (x ′ ). Let ϕ : PHf(x ′ )/Ke → PHf(x)/Ke be the natural
morphism. Then x ∈ Int(X ′ /X ) if and only if ϕ −1 (red(X , x)) = red(X ′ , x ′ ).
6.16 (Residues of PL functions). Let h: X → R be a PL function on a good strictly K-
analytic space X . For x ∈ X , Chambert–Loir and Ducros define a canonical invertible
sheaf Lh (x) on the ringed space red(X , x), which is called the residue of h at x. It can be
described as follows, using [CD12, Exemple 6.6.8]. Let U be a compact strictly analytic
neighbourhood of x. By 5.6, there exists a formal K ◦ -model (U, L) of (U, OX |U ) such that
h = − log k1kL on U. This L restricts to an invertible sheaf LU,x on VU,x , and we get an
invertible sheaf Lh (x) = π∗U,x LU,x on red(X , x).
  Let PL(X , x) denote the group of germs of PL functions on X at x; this is intrinsic to
the germ (X , x). Applying the above to germs of PL functions at x, we obtain a homo-
morphism L : PL(X , x) → Pic(red(X , x)), denoted h 7→ Lh (x).
Lemma 6.17. Let (X , x) be a good strictly K-analytic germ. If h ∈ PL(X , x) has the form
log | f | for an invertible function f , then Lh (x) = 0 in Pic(red(X , x)).
Proof. Choose a compact strictly analytic space X and a point x ∈ X representing (X , x)
such that there exists an invertible function f on X with h = log | f |. Since X is compact,
there exists a formal K ◦ -model X of X . Let L = OX · f , regarded as a subsheaf of the sheaf
U 7→ Γ (Uη , OX ) on X. Then k1kL = | f |−1 , and hence h = − log k1kL . Since L is trivial, so
is LX,x , so we conclude that Lh (x) = 0.                                                    

6.19 (Functoriality of residues). Let f : (X ′ , x ′ ) → (X , x) be a morphism of good strictly
K-analytic germs. For h ∈ PLΛ (X , x), we get h′ = h ◦ f ∈ PLΛ (X ′ , x ′ ). We have an induced
morphism fe : red(X ′ , x ′ ) → red(X , x), and Lh′ (x ′ ) = fe∗ Lh (x) by [CD12, 6.6.7]. A similar
statement holds for extension of scalars.

                          7. SEMIPOSITIVITY AND HARMONIC FUNCTIONS
   In this section, we study positivity conditions on piecewise linear functions. We will
introduce the crucial notion of a harmonic function, on which our theory of differential
forms rests. We consider a good strictly K-analytic space X and an additive subgroup
Λ ⊂ R. We will always assume that Λ = Z or that Λ is divisible.
   We can characterize semipositivity either in terms of formal geometry or residues of
Λ-PL functions, as elaborated in 6. A line bundle L on an admissible formal scheme X
is called nef (resp. numerically trivial) if its restriction to Xs has the same property. In
other words, the line bundle L is nef if the degree of L|C is nonnegative for every closed
integral curve C ⊂ Xs which is proper over K   e .5
   Let X be a formal K ◦ -model of a paracompact good strictly K-analytic space X . Recall
from 5.5 that M (X) denotes the group of models of OX , and that we have defined a
homomorphism h 7→ hL : M (X)Λ → PL(X )Λ . We will also use the residue Lh (x) of h at
x ∈ X from 6.16.
Proposition 7.1. Let h: X → R be a Λ-PL function and x ∈ X . The following are equivalent:
         (1) There exists a paracompact neighbourhood U of x in X , a formal K ◦ -model U of U,
             and a nef (resp. numerically trivial) element L ∈ M (U)Λ , such that h|U = hL .
         (2) The residue Lh (x) ∈ Pic(red(X , x))Λ is nef (resp. numerically trivial).
Proof. The first assertion implies the second by the description of Lh (x) in 6.16. We will
prove the nef case, as the numerically trivial case is identical. The following argument
is similar to [GK19, Proposition 6.5]. Suppose that Lh (x) is nef. By Corollary 5.9, there
exists a compact strictly analytic neighbourhood U of x, a formal K ◦ -model U of U, and an
element L ∈ M (U)Λ , such that h = hL . As in 6.16 we let VU,x be the closure of redU (x) and
we let LU,x be the restriction of L to VU,x . Then (VU,x , LU,x ) is a model of (red(X , x), Lh (x)),
so LU,x is nef by Lemma 6.11. Let W be a compact, strictly analytic neighbourhood of x
contained in the open set red−1 U
                                    (VU,x ). By [BL93a, Lemma 4.4], there exists an admissible
formal blowup f : U′ → U such that W = Wη for a formal open subset W ⊂ U′ . Note that
h|W = h f ∗ L|W . We claim that f ∗ L|W is nef.
     5
    If X is a proper K ◦ -model of a proper variety X over K and L is a line bundle on X with L = L |X
such that the restriction of L to the special fiber of X is nef, then L is a nef line bundle on X by [GM19,
4.8].

  By functoriality of reductions, the set-theoretic image of W under f is contained in

C is reduced, the schematic image of C is contained in VU,x , so we have a commutative
square of morphisms of ek-schemes:

Since LU,x is nef, we have f ∗ LU,x .C ≥ 0 by the projection formula.                       
Definition 7.2. A Λ-PL function h: X → R is called semipositive at a point x ∈ X if it
satisfies the equivalent conditions of Proposition 7.1, and it is harmonic at x if both h
and −h are semipositive at x. We say that h is semipositive if it is semipositive at all
points of X .
   A harmonic function on X is an R-PL function h: X → R which is harmonic at all points
of X . A Λ-PL harmonic function is called Λ-harmonic.
7.3. Some remarks:
   (1) A Λ-PL function h is harmonic at x ∈ X if and only if Lh (x) is numerically trivial.
       Equivalently, there exists a paracompact neighbourhood U of x, a formal K ◦ -
       model U of U, and a numerically trivial element L ∈ M (U)Λ , such that h = hL .
   (2) Semipositivity and harmonicity are insensitive to the group Λ, in the following
       sense: if h ∈ PLΛ (X ), then h is semipositive at a point x if and only if its image in
       PLR (X ) is semipositive at x.
   (3) Semipositivity and harmonicity of h at x is intrinsic to the germ of h in PLΛ (X , x).
       In particular, the semipositive (resp. harmonic) functions on X form a sheaf in
       the analytic topology. (They do not form a sheaf in the G-topology).
   (4) It follows from Proposition 7.1(1) that the locus of all points x ∈ X at which h is
       semipositive (resp. harmonic) is open.
   (5) The set of semipositive functions at a point forms a monoid (resp. a cone if
       Λ = R), and the set of harmonic functions at a point forms a group (resp. an
       R-subspace).
   (6) If h = log | f | for an invertible analytic function f in a neighbourhood of x, then h
       is harmonic at x by Lemma 6.17 (the converse does not hold: see Remark 15.10).
   (7) Harmonic functions generalize smooth functions in the same way that numerical
       equivalence generalizes rational equivalence in algebraic geometry.
Lemma 7.4. Suppose that X is paracompact. Let X be a formal K ◦ -model of X and let L ∈
M (X)Λ . Then hL is semipositive (resp. harmonic) if and only if L is nef (resp. numerically
trivial).
Proof. The harmonic case follows from the semipositive case. If L is nef then hL is semi-
positive by definition. Suppose then that h := hL is semipositive. Let C ⊂ Xs be an
integral, proper closed curve. By surjectivity of redX , there exists a point x ∈ X reducing
to the generic point of C. Then (C, L|C ) is a model of (red(X , x), Lh (x)), so L|C is nef by
Lemma 6.11. In particular, c1 (L).C ≥ 0, so L is nef.                                       
46                               W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

  Combined with 5.6, we see that a PL function h: X → R on a paracompact space X is
semipositive (resp. harmonic) if and only if there exists a formal K ◦ -model X of X and a
nef (resp. numerically trivial) line bundle L ∈ M (X) such that h = hL = − log k1kL .
  We have the following alternative characterization of harmonic functions. We do not
know if the same characterization holds for semipositive functions.
Proposition 7.5. Let h: X → R be a Λ-PL function and let x ∈ X . Then h is harmonic Pm at x
if and only if there exists a strictly analytic neighbourhood U of x such that h|U = i=1 λi hi
for Z-harmonic functions h1 , . . . , hm on U and λ1 , . . . , λm ∈ Λ.
Proof. The proposition is vacuous when Λ = Z, so assume Λ is divisible. The “if” direc-
tion is trivial. Suppose then thatPmh is harmonic at x. By Proposition 5.8, after shrinking
X , we may assume that h = i=1 λi hi for λ1 , . . . , λm ∈ Λ and h1 , . . . , hm ∈ PL(X ). By
Lemma 5.10, we may assume that   Pm λ1 , . . . , λm are Q-linearly independent. By Lemma 6.18,
the residue Lh (x) is equal to i=1 λi Lhi (x). Let V be a model for red(X , x) in the sense
of 6.2 such that each Lhi (x) is the pullback of a line bundle L V,i on V . Let C ⊂ V be an inte-
                                                                            Pm
gral, proper closed curve. Since Lh (x) is numerically trivial, we have i=1 λi c1 (L V,i (x)).C =
0. But c1 (L V,i (x)).C ∈ Z for each i, so by linear independence, we have c1 (L V,i (x)).C = 0
for each i. Thus L V,i is numerically trivial, so hi is harmonic.                              

Definition 7.8. Let h: X → R be an R-PL function. We say that h is Fubini–Study at a
point x ∈ X if Lh (x) is semiample. We say that h is locally Fubini–Study or LFS if it is
Fubini–Study at every point of X .
  Clearly if h is Fubini–Study at x then it is semipositive at x.
7.9. It follows from [CD12, Proposition 6.8.2] that h is Fubini–Study at x if and only if
there is an open neighbourhood U of x such that h|U is an R≥0 -linear combination of func-
tions of the form max{− log | f1 |, . . . , − log | f n |} for invertible analytic functions f1 , . . . , f n
on U.
  The following proposition can be seen as a local analogue of the fact that one can
approximate a nef Q-line bundle by ample Q-line bundles on a projective variety.
Proposition 7.10. Let h: X → R be an R-PL function that is semipositive at a point x ∈

neighbourhood of x on which h′ is defined. Then there is a formal K ◦ -model U of U and
a line bundle L′ on U with h′ = hL′ . By Lemma 6.14, there is a compact strictly analytic
neighbourhood U ′ of x and a formal K ◦ -model U′ of U ′ such that the closure V ′ := VU′ ,x
of redU′ (x) dominates V . We may assume that U ′ ⊂ U and that this inclusion extends to
a morphism f : U′ → U by using Raynaud’s theorem. Let L ′ |V ′ be the pull-back of L ′ with
respect to V ′ → V . Then L ′ |V ′ and f ∗ (L′ )|V ′ are both models for Lh′ (x). We conclude that
there is a dominating model V ′′ of red(X , x) such that the pull-backs of L ′ |V ′ and f ∗ (L′ )|V ′
to V ′′ agree. Using Lemma 6.14 again and the arguments above, we may assume that
V ′′ = VU′′ ,x for a formal K ◦ -model U′′ of a compact strictly analytic neighbourhood U ′′
of x contained in U ′ such that the inclusion U ′′ ⊂ U ′ extends to a morphism U′′ → U′ .
Moreover, we may assume that U ′′ ⊂ X extends to a morphism U′′ → X. Replacing X by
U′′ and L, L′ by their pull-backs to U′′ , we put ourselves in the following situation:
     (1) The closure V ′ of redX (x) in Xs dominates V .
     (2) There is L ∈ M (X)R and L ∈ Pic(V )R such that h = hL and L|V ′ is the pullback of
         L under V ′ → V , and such that (V, L) is a model of Lh (x).
     (3) There is a line bundle L′ on X and an ample line bundle L ′ on V such that h′ = hL′
         and L′ |V ′ is the pullback of L ′ under V ′ → V .
We recycle notation now and let U := red−1       X
                                                     (V ′ ). By anticontinuity of the reduction map,
the set U is an open neighbourhood of x. For ǫ ∈ Q>0 let Lǫ := L + ǫ L ′ , Lǫ := L + ǫ L′ ,
and hǫ := hLǫ = h + ǫh′ . Then hǫ is an R-PL function on X and Lǫ |V ′ is the pullback of Lǫ
under V ′ → V . Note that hǫ → h uniformly as ǫ → 0. If h is semipositive at x then L is
nef by Lemma 6.11, so Lǫ is ample as a consequence of Kleiman’s theorem, see [Laz04,
Corollary 1.4.10]. It follows that Lǫ |V ′ is semiample. If y ∈ U then redX ( y) ∈ V ′ , so the
closure Y of redX ( y) in Xs is contained in V ′ . Since h = hL and h′ = hL′ on all of X ,
the pair (Y, Lǫ |Y ) is a model of Lhǫ ( y) by the construction in 6.16. Since Lǫ |Y is again
semiample, we see that hǫ is Fubini–Study at y. Thus hǫ is LFS on U.                                 
Remark 7.11. Let X be a proper scheme over K. It is shown in [GM19, Section 5] that
a pointwise limit of semipositive Q-PL metrics is semipositive. We expect the analogous
result to hold in our situation. In particular, we expect the converse of Proposition 7.10
to hold true: namely, if an R-PL function h is a uniform limit of R-PL LFS functions on
a strictly analytic neighbourhood U of an interior point x, then h is semipositive at x.
This would imply that if h and h′ are R-PL functions that are semipositive at x, then so is
max{h, h′ }. Indeed, we can write h = lim hi and h′ = lim h′i for R-PL LFS functions hi , h′i
in a neighbourhood U of x. By 7.9, the functions max{hi , h′i } are R-PL LFS functions on
U, and we have max{h, h′ } = lim max{hi , h′i }.
   Now we relate semipositivity to positive forms and currents. See 2.8 for the definitions
of positivity based on the corresponding notions for Lagerberg forms and Lagerberg cur-
rents introduced in 2.7. The next result serves as a replacement for [CD12, Lemmas 5.2.3
and 5.3.3] where the result is claimed for strongly positive forms; this is not correct, as
explained in [BGJK21a, Example 2.3.6].
                FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                             49

Lemma 7.12. Let X be a Hausdorff analytic space over K and let ω ∈ Asm,c   (X ) be symmetric.
                                         p,p

Proof. The support of ω is compact and hence has a paracompact open neighbourhood

                              p,p

7.13 (Continuous Plurisubharmonic Functions). For simplicity, we suppose that X is a
boundaryless6 good separated strictly analytic space to use currents on X as in 2.8. Using
the theory of integration from [CD12, 3.7–3.10], every continuous real function h on X
has an associated current [h] given by
                                                       Z
                               [h]: Asm,c (X ) −→ R ,        ω 7→            h·ω
                                                                         X
where the integral is defined by a limit process using that h is a uniform limit of smooth
functions on a neighbourhood of the support of ω (see [CD12, Corollaire 5.4.7, 5.4.8]).
We say that h is plurisubharmonic (short: psh) if the current d′ d′′ [h] is positive. A parti-
tion of unity argument shows that plurisubharmonicity is a local notion on X (see [CD12,
Lemme 5.5.2]).
   By [CD12, Lemme 5.5.3], a smooth function h is psh if and only if every x ∈ X has a
                                                                                r,an
compact strictly analytic neighbourhood V with a moment map ϕ : V → Gm               such that
h|V = u◦ϕtrop for a continuous function u on ϕtrop (V ) which restricts to a convex function
on each face. This characterization shows that smooth psh functions admit pull-backs
with respect to morphisms.
Theorem 7.14. Let X be a good separated strictly analytic space over K and let h: X → R
be a semipositive R-PL function. Then h restricts to a psh function on X \ ∂ X .
Proof. By restriction to X \ ∂ X , we may assume that X is boundaryless. The claim is local
in X and so we may assume that h is a uniform limit of R-PL LFS functions hi on X by
using Proposition 7.10. It follows from 7.9 and from [CD12, Corollaire 6.3.2] that an
R-PL locally Fubini–Study function is locally a uniform limit of smooth psh functions and
hence hi is psh. For any positive form ω ∈ Asm,c (X ), we deduce from uniform convergence
that                                 Z                  Z
                      〈ω, d′ d′′ [h]〉 =        h · d′ d′′ ω = lim       hi · d′ d′′ ω ≥ 0.

along the boundary. Note that Chambert-Loir and Ducros also omit the boundary in their definition of

Remark 7.16. In this section, we have focused on the concept of semipositive and har-
monic functions. In fact, all the results immediately transfer to Λ-PL metrics k k of a
line bundle L over a good strictly analytic space X . Indeed, the choice of a local frame s
gives rise to a Λ-PL function h := − log ksk, to which we can apply the above results. Then
Theorem 7.14 yields that on a good separated strictly analytic space X every semipositive
R-PL metric of L has a positive first Chern current c1 (L, k k) as defined in [CD12, 6.4.1].
The same holds for uniform limits of such metrics on L which includes all the metrics
considered by Zhang [Zha95] over projective varieties.

                  8. PIECEWISE LINEAR AND HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS
  In this section, we assume that X is a compact good strictly K-analytic space of pure
dimension d. We will define a piecewise linear tropicalization map to be a tuple h =
(h1 , . . . , hn) of PL functions on X . We will endow h(X ) ⊂ Rn with the structure of a
weighted (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex, which is canonical up to subdivision. The difficult
part is defining weights on h(X ), which we will do G-locally using the results of 4.
Definition 8.1. A piecewise linear (PL) tropicalization is a map
                                    h = (h1 , . . . , hn): X −→ Rn
where h1 , . . . , hn are piecewise linear functions. It is called a harmonic tropicalization if
in addition all the hi are harmonic (which means that all of the hi are Z-harmonic). It is
called a smooth tropicalization map if hi = log | f i | for f i ∈ Γ (X , OX× ).
8.2. Remarks:
                                                                                     n,an
   (1) A smooth tropicalization map has the form ϕtrop for a moment map ϕ : X → Gm        .
       These are the tropicalizations considered in [CD12].
   (2) A smooth tropicalization map is harmonic by Remark 7.3(6).
   (3) A piecewise linear tropicalization is G-locally a smooth tropicalization map.
                  FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                                  51

    (4) Piecewise linear, harmonic, and smooth tropicalization maps all pull back under
        morphisms of analytic spaces and arbitrary change of base: see Lemma 5.4 and
        Proposition 7.6.
Proposition 8.3. A function h: X → R is piecewise linear if and only if each x ∈ X has a
strictly affinoid neighbourhood U such that h|U = F ◦ h′ for a smooth tropicalization map
h′ : U → Rn and a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear function F : h′ (U) → R.

8.4 (Covering by a Smooth Tropicalization Map). Let h: X → Rn be a piecewise linear
tropicalization map. Applying Proposition 8.3 to the coordinate functions h1 , . . . , hn , we
get locally on X a smooth tropicalization map h′ : U → Rm and a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear
map F : h′ (U) → Rn with h = F ◦ h′ on U.
Definition 8.5. A piecewise linear tropicalization h: X → Rn is covered by a smooth trop-
icalization map h′ : U → Rm on a compact strictly analytic subdomain U ⊂ X if there is a
piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear function F : h′ (U) → Rn such that h = F ◦ h′ .
  It follows from 8.4 that locally on X , a piecewise linear tropicalization is always covered
by a smooth tropicalization map.
52                               W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

map. There is a (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex Π of dimension at most d with support h(X )
such that h(∂ X ) is contained in a subcomplex of dimension at most d − 1. This follows
from 3.2 by restricting h to any G-open piece where h is a smooth tropicalization map.
Explicitly, there is a finite G-covering (X j ) j∈J of X by compact strictly analytic subdomains
such that h|X j is the smooth tropicalization map associated to a moment map ϕ j : X j →
  n,an

Gm     . Then (ϕ j )trop (X j ) is a finite union of (Z, Γ )-polytopes, and we have ∂ X ⊂ j∈J ∂ X j .

8.8. Some remarks:
     (1) By Proposition 4.9, the quantity mh (X , x) does not depend on the choice of U or
         ϕ.
     (2) Suppose that h is smooth over ω ∈ h(X ). Let U ⊂ X be a compact strictly analytic
         domain such that ω ∈    / h(∂ U). Then h−1 (ω) ∩ U = h−1 (ω) ∩ Int(U) is open and
         closed in h−1 (ω). Since h−1 (ω) can be covered by finitely many such U, it follows
         from Remark 4.2(7) that mh (X , ω) is a nonnegative integer.
     (3) By 8.6, there is a (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex Π of dimension at most d with support
         h(X ) such that h is smooth over the complement of a subcomplex of dimension at
         most d −1. (Choose Π such that each h(∂ X i ) is contained in such a subcomplex.)
     (4) If h is smooth over ω ∈ h(X ) and h(X ) has dimension d at ω, then mh (X , ω) > 0
         by Remark 4.2(7).
     (5) If h is itself a smooth tropicalization map, i.e., if h = ϕtrop for a moment map
                     n,an
         ϕ : X → Gm       , then mh (X , x) = mϕ (X , x) and mh (X , ω) = mϕ (X , ω) for all x ∈
         Int(X ) and ω ∈ h(X ) \ h(∂ X ).
   In order to pass from points to faces, we need the following consequence of Proposi-
tion 4.11.

Definition 8.10 (Weights on PL tropicalizations). Let h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization.
Choose a (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex Π with support h(X ) such that h is smooth over the
complement of a subcomplex of dimension at most d − 1, as in Remark 8.8(3). Let σ be
a d-dimensional face of Π. Then we define the tropical weight of σ by mσ := mh (X , σ) :=
mh (X , ω) for any ω ∈ relint(σ). This is well-defined by Proposition 8.9.
  Let Πd ⊂ Π be the set of d-dimensional faces of Π. We consider the d-dimensional
part h(X )d of h(X ) as the weighted (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex (Πd , m), well-defined up to
subdivision.
  Tropical weights are a “geometric” notion, in the following sense.
Proposition 8.11. Let K ′ /K be an extension of non-Archimedean fields, let X ′ = X ⊗
                                                                                    b K K′
with structure morphism π: X ′ → X , let h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization, and let h′ =
h ◦ π. Then h′ (X ′ )d = h(X )d as weighted (Z, Γ )-polytopal complexes.
Proof. This is an immediate consequence of Proposition 4.8.                                           
8.12 (Change of tropicalization). Let h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization and let L : Rn →
Rm be a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear map. Then h′ := L ◦ h: X → Rm is a PL tropicalization.
Indeed, choosing a (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex structure Π on h(X ) such that L is (Z, Γ )-
linear on every face of Π, we see that G-locally on X , each coordinate of h′ is a Z-linear
combination of the coordinates of h plus a scalar in v(K × ).
   In the following, we will use the results of Ducros and of 4 to introduce an intrin-
sic closed subset S(X , h)d of X with a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear structure which covers the
tropical variety h(X )d , and we will endow S(X , h)d with canonical weights.
8.13 (The d-skeleton of a PL tropicalization). Let h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization map.
The d-skeleton of h is the subset

Lemma 8.14. Let Q : Zn → Zm be a homomorphism such that Q R is injective on every d-
dimensional face of h(X ). Then we have Sh (X )d = SQR ◦h (X )d .
Proof. Setting L := Q R , we have seen in 8.12 that h′ := L ◦ h is a PL tropicalization.
By definition, the d-skeleton Sh (X )d is the set of x ∈ X such that dim h(X , x) = d and
similarly for h′ . Since Q R is injective on every d-dimensional face of h(X ), we have
dim h(X , x) = dim h′ (X , x) for every x ∈ X . This proves Sh (X )d = Sh′ (X )d .      
8.15 (Weights on the d-skeleton). We continue with the notation in Remark 8.13. Af-

that

        face of Π.
    (3) Every h(∂ X j ) is contained in subcomplex of Π of dimension at most d − 1.
For a d-dimensional face ∆ of Sh (X ), there is j such that ∆ is a face of Sϕ j (X j ). For
x ∈ relint(∆), we conclude that mh (X , x) = mϕ j (X , x) is the weight mϕ j (X j , ∆) of the d-
dimensional face ∆ of Sϕ j (X j ) considered in Remark 4.13 and hence does not depend on
the choice of x ∈ relint(∆). Defining m∆ := mh (X , ∆) := mh (X , x), this makes Sh (X )d into
a weighted abstract (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex. The weighted polytopal complex h(X )d is
the push-forward of the weighted polytopal complex Sh (X )d , i.e. for any d-dimensional

Lemma 8.16 (Functoriality of the d-skeleton). Let f : X ′ → X be a morphism of compact
good strictly analytic spaces of pure dimension d and let h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization.
Then h′ := h ◦ f is a PL tropicalization with f (S(X ′ , h′ )d ) ⊂ S(X , h)d inducing a piecewise
(Z, Γ )-linear map Sh′ (X ′ )d → Sh (X ) which is finite to one.
Proof. Using the characterization of the d-skeleton by germs of tropicalizations, it is clear
that f (Sh′ (X ′ )d ) ⊂ Sh (X )d . Since h′ : Sh′ (X ′ )d → Rn factorizes through h: Sh (X )d → Rn
and both are finite-to-one PL maps which determine the PL-structure on the respective
d-skeletons, we deduce the claim.                                                               
Remark 8.17. We have seen in Proposition 3.12(8) that the preimage of the tropical
skeleton is the tropical skeleton. However, in the setting of Lemma 8.16, such an identity
(8.17.1)                           Sh′ (X ′ )d = f −1 (S(X , h)d )

   However, if f is open (in particular, this applies if f is finite and flat by [Ber93, Propo-
sition 3.2.7], then (8.17.1) is true. This follows from the definition of the d-skeleton in
terms of germs of tropicalizations.

  The following result will be used to define weights on germs of PL tropicalizations.
Lemma 8.21. Let h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization. For x ∈ X , there is a compact strictly
analytic neighbourhood V (x) of x such that for any compact strictly analytic neighbourhood
W ⊂ V (x) of x, the weighted germs of h(V (x))d and h(W )d at h(x) agree.
56                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

Proof. We choose compatible face structures on Sh (X )d and on h(X )d as in 8.15. The
faces of Sh (X )d not containing x form a closed subset A of X . Let V be any compact
strictly analytic neighbourhood of x with V ∩ A = ;. We have seen in 8.13 that the map f
restricts to a surjective piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear map Sh (X )d → h(X )dS
                                                                        which is finite to one.
We conclude that the germ of h(V )d at h(x) agrees with the germ h(∆) at h(x) where
∆ ranges over all d-dimensional faces of Sh (X )d containing x. Moreover, it follows from
(8.15.1) that the tropical P multiplicity mσ in a d-dimensional face of h(V )d containing
h(x) is given by mσ = ∆ m∆ , where ∆ ranges over all d-dimensional faces of Sh (X )d
with x ∈ ∆ and h(∆) = σ. All this does not depend on the particular choice of V . We
pick for V (x) any compact strictly analytic neighbourhood of x with V (x) ∩ A = ;. Then
the claim follows by using this independence for W ⊂ V (x).                                  
8.22 (Tropical variety of a germ). Let h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization, let x ∈ X , and
let V (x) be a compact strictly analytic neighbourhood of x as in Lemma 8.21. We define
the tropical variety h(X , x) of the germ (X , x) as the germ of h(V (x)) at h(x). It is the germ
of a fan centered at h(x). We use the weights on h(V (x)) to endow the d-dimensional
part h(X , x)d of the germ h(X , x) with weights on its d-dimensional faces. This makes
h(X , x)d into a weighted fan.
Remark 8.23. Let h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization. It follows from the definitions that
for any ω ∈ Rn , we have the identity
                                              X

                                                   x∈X
                                                 h(x)=ω

of weighted fans in ω, well-defined up to subdivision.
Remark 8.24 (Weights on germs via the d-skeleton). Let x ∈ X . If x ∈         / Sh (X )d then
dim h(X , x) < d, so h(X , x)d = 0 as a weighted fan. If x ∈ Sh (X )d , then it follows from
(8.15.1) that h(X , x)d is the push-forward of the weighted germ of Sh (X )d at x.
   For a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear map L of piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear spaces, we have a push-
forward L∗ of weighted (Z, Γ )-polyhedral complexes, well-defined up to subdivision (see
2.5). Tropical varieties satisfy the following functoriality property with respect to such
L.

of weighted fans (up to subdivision). Moreover, we have L∗ (h(X )d ) = h′ (X )d as weighted
(Z, Γ )-polytopal complexes (up to subdivision).

8.26 (Tropicalization and Pushforward of Cycles). Recall from 4.4 that we have defined
the fundamental cycle [X ] of an analytic space X . More generally, a cycle on X is a finite
sum of irreducible Zariski-closed subsets of X . Let h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization. By
linearity, we define a weighted (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex h(Z) for all cycles Z on X using
Definition 8.10 on prime cycles. It follows from Lemma 4.5 that h([X ]) = h(X )d .
    Given a proper morphism f : X ′ → X of strictly analytic spaces, a proper push-forward
of cycles was defined in [Gub98, 2.6]. By linearity and by restriction to components, it
is enough to define f∗ ([X ′ ]) for a proper surjective morphism of irreducible and reduced
compact strictly analytic spaces. If dim(X ) < dim(X ′ ), then we set f∗ ([X ′ ]) = 0. Other-
wise, we have dim(X ) = dim(X ′ ) and f is finite over the complement of a Zariski closed
subset of X of dimension < d and hence has a a well-defined degree δ. Then we set
f∗ ([X ′ ]) = δ [X ].

   In the following, we discuss generalizations of the Sturmfels–Tevelev formula in trop-
ical geometry. First, we give an analogue for d-skeletons.

Proposition 8.27. Let f : X ′ → X be a surjective proper morphism of d-dimensional com-
pact, irreducible, reduced, good strictly analytic spaces. Let δ be the degree of f . Let
h: X → Rn be a PL tropicalization and let h′ := h ◦ f . Recall from 8.16 that the restriction
of f gives a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear map F : Sh′ (X ′ )d → Sh (X )d . Then we have the identity

(8.27.1)                              F∗ (Sh′ (X ′ )d ) = δ · Sh (X )d

of weighted abstract (Z, Γ )-polytopal complexes up to subdivision.
58                                   W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

Proof. We may prove the statement locally in x ∈ X . Clearly, we may asume that x ∈
relint(∆) for a d-dimensional face ∆ of Sh (X )d . Since X is reduced, we have seen in Re-

x has a compact strictly analytic neighbourhood U so that f −1 (U) → U is finite and flat.
Replacing f by this restriction, we may assume that f is a finite flat morphism. It fol-
lows from Remark 8.17 that f −1 (Sh (X )d ) = Sh′ (X ′ )d . There are finitely many preimages
y1 , . . . , yr ∈ X ′ of x with respect to f and we conclude that all y j ∈ Sh′ (X ′ )d .
    We choose compatible face structures on Sh′ (X ′ )d , Sh (X )d and h(X ) = h′ (X ′ ) as in 8.19.
We may assume that ∆ is such a face of Sh (X )d . It follows from the above that for any
y j there is a unique face ∆ j of Sh′ (X ′ )d such that f (∆ j ) = ∆ and y j ∈ relint(∆ j ). For the
                                                                   Pr

   The following proposition generalizes the original formula given in [ST08] for trivial
valuations and in [BPR16] for a general non-archimededean field, both of which treat
closed subschemes of a torus.
Proposition 8.28 (Sturmfels–Tevelev Formula). Let f : X ′ → X be a proper morphism of
compact, good, strictly K-analytic spaces. Assume we are given a commutative square
                                                          h′

where h and h′ are PL tropicalizations and L is piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear. Then for any cycle
Z ′ on X ′ , we have
(8.28.1)                                 h( f∗ (Z ′ )d ) = L∗ (h′ (Z ′ )d )
as weighted (Z, Γ )-polytopal complexes (up to subdivision).
Proof. We may assume that L is the identity map on Rn by Proposition 8.25. By linearity
we may assume that Z ′ = X ′ , so that X ′ is reduced and irreducible. Let d = dim(X ′ ). We
may replace X by f (X ′ ) to assume f is surjective and X is reduced. If dim(X ) < dim(X ′ )
then f∗ [X ′ ] = 0 and dim(h′ (X ′ )) < d, so that h′ (X ′ )d = 0 and the claim follows. So we
may suppose then that dim(X ) = dim(X ′ ) and we denote by δ the degree of f . Using the
restriction F : Sh′ (X ′ )d → Sh (X )d , Proposition 8.27.1 and Remark 8.24 twice, we get

Since the right hand side agrees with h∗ ( f∗ [X ′ ]), this proves (8.28.1).                                     

                              9. THE BALANCING CONDITION
  In this section, we prove the balancing condition for harmonic tropicalizations of com-
pact good strictly analytic spaces over K. We start with some polyhedral geometry using
the notions from 2.5. We fix a subgroup Γ of R which will be later the value group of K.
9.1 (The Balancing Condition). We first recall the balancing condition for a weighted
(Z, Γ )-polyhedral complex Π of dimension d in Rm , with weights mσ ∈ Z for each d-
dimensional face σ ∈ Π. For any face σ ∈ Π, let Lσ be the linear subspace of Rm generated
by {ω − ρ | ω, ρ ∈ σ}. The lattice N := Zm induces a canonical lattice Nσ := Lσ ∩ N in
Lσ . For each face τ of σ of codimension 1, we choose ωσ,τ ∈ Nσ such that ωσ,τ + Nτ
generates the lattice Nσ /Nτ of rank 1. Note that the generator ωσ,τ + Nτ is determined
up to sign; we fix the sign by requiring that ωσ,τ points inwards at the boundary τ of σ.
Then we say that Π is balanced at a face τ ∈ Π of dimension d − 1 if we have
                                     X
                                         mσ ωσ,τ ∈ Lτ ,
                                        σ≻τ

where σ ranges over all d-dimensional faces of Π containing τ. We say that Π is balanced
if it is balanced at every (d − 1)-dimensional face τ ∈ Π. In this case, the weighted
polyhedral complex is called a tropical cycle of dimension d.
   In the following, we consider a weighted (Z, Γ )-polyhedral complex Π in Rm of dimen-
sion d. Let ϕ : |Π| → R be a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear function with tropical Weil divisor
(corner locus) div(ϕ) (see [AR10, Section 6]). By subdividing Π, we may assume that
the restriction of ϕ to every face σ ∈ Π is (Z, Γ )-linear. Let Π′ := ΓΠ (ϕ) be the graph of
ϕ with the same weights as Π. We denote by ι : Π → Π′ the canonical isomorphism.
60                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

Proposition 9.2. The graph Π′ is balanced if and only if Π is balanced and div(ϕ) = 0.
Proof. We always denote by τ′ ∈ Π′ the face ι(τ) corresponding to τ ∈ Π and vice versa.

a linear function on τ, i.e., that both τ′ and τ contain the origin and ϕ(0) = 0. Then Π′
is balanced at τ′ if and only if

                                        σ′ ≻τ′

Lemma 9.3. Let Π be a tropical cycle in Rm and let ϕ, ψ: Π → R be piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear
functions. Let Π′ = ΓΠ (ϕ) be the graph of ϕ and let π: Π′ → Π be the canonical map. If the
tropical Weil divisor div(ψ) is equal to zero, then div(π∗(ψ)) = 0 on Π′ .
Proof. Let τ′ be a face of Π′ of codimension 1, corresponding to a (Z, Γ )-polyhedron τ of
Π. By definition of a tropical Weil divisor, the multiplicity of τ′ in div(π∗ ψ) is
     X          ∂ π∗ ψ           ∂ π∗ ψ             X      ∂ψ             ∂ψ

where σ′ (resp. σ) is ranging over all d-dimensional faces of Π′ (resp. Π) containing τ′
(resp. τ). This proves the claim.                                                     
Corollary 9.4. Let Π be a tropical cycle in Rm of dimension d and let L : Π → Rn be a
piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear map. Suppose that L(x) = (ϕ1 (x), . . . , ϕn (x)) for piecewise (Z, Γ )-
linear functions ϕi on Π with div(ϕi ) = 0. Then the graph Π′ := ΓΠ (L) is a tropical cycle in
Rm × Rn of dimension d, endowed with the weights given by transport of structure via the
canonical isomorphism ι : Π → Π′ . Moreover, we have L = p2 ◦ ι for the second projection
p2 : Rm × Rn → Rn .
Proof. This follows from an inductive application of Proposition 9.2 and Lemma 9.3.           
               FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                  61

   Now we apply the above considerations to tropicalizations over a non-trivially valued
non-Archimedean field K with additive value group Γ ⊂ R. In the following, we assume
that X is a compact good strictly analytic space of pure dimension d.
   For a smooth tropicalization map F : X → Rm , Chambert-Loir and Ducros have shown
that that the d-dimensional part F (X )d of the tropical variety F (X ) is balanced outside
F (∂ X ). We have here the following immediate consequence of their result:
Lemma 9.5. Let F : X → Rm be a smooth tropicalization map. For x ∈ X \ ∂ X , the germ
F (X , x)d from 8.22 is a balanced fan.
Proof. By Lemma 8.21, there is a compact strictly analytic neighbourhood V (x) of x such
that the weighted germ F (X , x)d at x agrees with the weighted germ of F (V (x))d at F (x).
By [CD12], the latter is balanced, proving the claim.                                     
Proposition 9.6. Let F : X → Rm be a smooth tropicalization map, let x ∈ Int(X ), and let
ϕ be a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear function on F (X , x) such that h := ϕ ◦ F is harmonic in a
neighbourhood of x. Then the tropical Weil divisor div(ϕ, x) of ϕ on the fan F (X , x)d is
equal to zero.
Proof. By Corollary 7.15, after potentially shrinking X , we have d′ d′′ [h] = 0 on Int(X ) =
X \∂ X . Since ∂ X is closed in X , it follows from Lemma 8.21 that there is a strictly affinoid
neighbourhood W of X contained in Int(X ) such that the weighted germ of F (W )d in F (x)
agrees with F (V, x)d . Clearly, we may also assume that ϕ is defined on F (W ) and that h
is harmonic on W . Note that d′ d′′ [h] = 0 on W \ ∂ W . We may assume that x ∈ S(X , x)d ;
otherwise the weighted fan F (X , x)d = F (W, x)d is 0 (see Remark 8.24). Then x is an
Abhyankar point of X as seen in 8.13. By [CD12, Proposition 3.4.4], there is a strictly
affinoid neighbourhood U of x contained in Int(W ) ⊂ Int(X ) and a smooth tropicalization
map G0 : U → Rn0 with G0 (∂ U) ∩ G0 ({x}) = ;. Setting n = m + n0 and G = (F, G0 ): U →
Rn , we have G(∂ U) ∩ G({x}) = ; and F |U = L ◦ G, where L : Rm × Rn0 → Rm is the
projection onto the first factor. The piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear function ψ := ϕ ◦ L on G(U)
satisfies ψ ◦ G = h|U . For any Lagerberg form ω ∈ Ad,d (G(U)) with compact support in
the open neighbourhood Ω := G(U) \ G(∂ U) of G(x) in G(U), we have the identity
                           〈ω, d′ d′′ [ψ]〉 = 〈G ∗(ω), d′ d′′ [h]〉 = 0,
and hence d′ d′′ [ψ|Ω ] = 0. Using that x 6∈ ∂ U, Theorem 3.10 shows that the germ G(U, x)
is a fan of pure dimension d, so we may shrink the open neighbourhood Ω of G(x) such
that Ω generates the fan G(U, x). Since x 6∈ ∂ U, Lemma 9.5 yields that the fan G(U, x)d
is balanced. The tropical Poincaré–Lelong formula (see 2.7) shows that d′ d′′ [ψ|Ω ] is the
current of integration over the tropical Weil divisor div(ψ|Ω ). Using d′ d′′ [ψ|Ω ] = 0, we
deduce that div(ψ|Ω ) = 0, and hence the tropical divisor div(ψ, x) on the fan G(U, x) =
G(U, x)d is zero. By Proposition 8.25, we have L∗ (G(U, x)d ) = F (X , x)d and hence the
projection formula [AR10, Proposition 7.7] gives div(ϕ, x) = L∗ (div(ψ, x)) = 0, which
proves the claim.                                                                          
   We will generalize the balancing result of Chambert-Loir and Ducros in Lemma 9.5 to
a harmonic tropicalization map h: X → Rn . We recall from 8.22 that a germ (X , x) gives
rise to a canonical weighted rational fan h(X , x)d of pure dimension d. In Definition 8.10,
we have seen that h(X )d is canonically a weighted (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex.
62                                W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

Theorem 9.7. Let X be a good compact strictly analytic space of pure dimension d, and let
h: X → Rn be a harmonic tropicalization map. Then we have the following properties:
    (1) For any x ∈ Int(X ), the weighted fan h(X , x)d of the germ (X , x) is balanced.
    (2) The weighted polytopal complex h(X )d satisfies the balancing condition in every
        (d − 1)-dimensional face not contained in h(Sh (X )d ∩∂ X ).
   Note here that balanced might mean that there simply are no d-dimensional faces. In a
previous version, we required in (2) only that the (d − 1)-dimensional face omits h(∂ X ).
An argument of Andreas Mihatsch allows us to replace the latter by h(Sh (X )d ∩∂ X ) where
Sh (X )d is the d-skeleton of h from 8.13.
Proof. Let x ∈ Int(X ). By 8.4, there is a compact strictly analytic subdomain W of X
which is a neighbourhood of x, a smooth tropicalization map F : W → Rm and a piecewise
(Z, Γ )-linear map L : F (W ) → Rn such that h = L ◦ F . By Lemma 9.5, the fan F (W, x)d is
balanced.
   Suppose that L = (ϕ1 , . . . , ϕn ) for piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear functions ϕ1 , . . . , ϕn : F (W ) →
R. Since h is harmonic, Proposition 9.6 shows that the tropical Weil divisor of ϕi is zero in
a neighbourhood of ω := F (x). We transfer F (X , x) into Rm ×Rn by the graph embedding
ι of L. Then Corollary 9.4 shows that ι∗ (F (X , x)d ) is a balanced fan. It follows from
(8.25.1) that
                        (p2 )∗ (ι∗ (F (X , x)d )) = L∗ (F (W, x)d ) = h(X , x)d .
Since p2 is a linear map, we conclude from [GKM09, Proposition 2.25] that h(X , x)d is
balanced, proving the first claim.
   To prove the second claim, let τ be any (d −1)-dimensional face of h(X )d not contained
in h(Sh (X )d ∩∂ X ). For ω ∈ relint(τ), we have to show that the weighted germ of h(X )d
in ω is balanced. By Remark 8.23, the weighted germ of h(X )d in ω is the sum of the
weighted germs h(X , x)d with x ranging over h−1 (ω). Note that we choose always the
face structure on h(X )d as in 8.15 and hence ω ∈           / h(Sh (X )d ∩ ∂ X ). If x ∈ h−1 (ω) then
either x ∈ ∂ X \ Sh (X )d , in which case h(X , x)d = 0 by the definition of the d-skeleton in
8.13, or x ∈ Sh (X )d \ ∂ X , in which case h(X , x)d is balanced by the first claim.                  
Remark 9.8. In a first version of this paper, we have stated here that a harmonic R-PL
function satisfies the local maximum principle on X \∂ X . We will shift this to a subsequent
paper, where we prove this more generally for semipositive R-PL functions.

                                    10. DIFFERENTIAL FORMS
   In this section, we will generalize the smooth differential forms of Chambert-Loir and
Ducros [CD12] on a good strictly analytic space X over the non-trivially valued non-
Archimedean field K. The idea is to replace smooth tropicalization maps by harmonic
tropicalization maps, thus yielding weakly smooth forms. Many properties of smooth
differential forms are also true for weakly smooth forms, but we will see later that weakly
smooth forms are better behaved for cohomological questions.
10.1 (Lagerberg forms). Let S be a piecewise Z-linear space in Rn , as in 2.5. A typical
example is the tropical variety of a compact strictly analytic space: see 3.2 in case of a
smooth tropicalization map and 8.6 for the generalization to PL tropicalizations. In 2.7,
                FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                       63

we have recalled the sheaf A•,• of Lagerberg forms on S; they form a sheaf of differential
bigraded R-algebras with respect to differentials d′ and d′′ . For a piecewise Z-linear space
T in Rm and an affine Z-linear map F : Rm → Rn with F (T ) ⊂ S, there is a functorial pull-
back F ∗ : AS → F∗ (A T ).
10.2 (Piecewise Smooth Forms). Also important for us is the sheaf of piecewise smooth
forms on the piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear space S, which we denote by Aps . Here, a piecewise
smooth form α on an open subset Ω of S is given in a neighbourhood Ω p of any point
p ∈ Ω by a (Z, Γ )-polytopal complex Π with support S and α∆ ∈ A∆ (∆ ∩ Ω p ) for ∆ ∈ Π
such that α∆ restricts to ατ for every face τ of ∆. We identify two piecewise smooth
forms α, α′ if for every p ∈ Ω, we have α∆ = α′∆′ on Ω p ∩ Ω′p ∩ ∆ ∩ ∆′ for every ∆ ∈ Π and
every ∆′ ∈ Π′ for local representations of α, α′ in p. We have natural differentials d′P and
d′′P on Aps called polyhedral derivatives. They are induced by the differentials d′ , d′′ on the
smooth pieces of a piecewise smooth form. We get a bigraded sheaf A•,•      ps
                                                                               of differential
R-algebras on S containing A as a subsheaf. We refer to [GK17, Definition 3.10] for a
similar construction using Z-polyhedral complexes instead of (Z, Γ )-polytopal complexes.
For a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear map F : T → S of (Z, Γ )-polytopal sets, there is a functorial
pull-back F ∗ : AS,ps → F∗ (A T,ps ).

Definition 10.3 (Preforms). Let U be an open subset of X whose closure U is a compact
strictly analytic domain. A weakly smooth preform of type (p, q) on U is given by a tuple
                                                                           p,q
(h, α), where h: U → Rn is a harmonic tropicalization map and α ∈ A P (P) for P := h(U).
Two tuples (h, α) and (h′ , α′ ) are considered equal if p1∗ α and p2∗ α′ agree on (h × h′ )(U),
                                                 ′
where p1 and p2 are the projections of Rn × Rn to the two factors.
Lemma 10.4. For a weakly smooth preform (h, α) on U, we have (h, α) = 0 if and only if
α = 0 in A(h(U)).
Proof. One direction is clear, so suppose that (h, α) = 0. This means that there is a
                                             ′
harmonic tropicalization map h′ : U → Rn such that p1∗ α = 0 on (h × h′ )(U). Since
p1 (h × h′ )(U) = h(U), we can cover h(U) by the images of polytopes in (h × h′ )(U), so
p1∗ α = 0 implies α = 0. See the proof of [CD12, Lemme 3.2.2] for more details.       
10.5 (Restriction of preforms). Let U ⊂ U ′ be open subsets of X whose closures U ⊂
U ′ are compact strictly analytic domains. The restriction of a weakly smooth preform
(h, α) on U ′ is the weakly smooth preform given by (h|U , α|h(U) ). The weakly smooth
preforms thus form a presheaf of bigraded R-algebras on the category of open subsets
whose closure is a compact strictly analytic domain. Note that such open sets form a base
for the analytic topology of our good strictly analytic space X .
10.6 (Wedge product and differentials). Let U ⊂ X be an open subset whose closure U
is a compact strictly analytic domain. Let (h, α) and (h′ , α′ ) be weakly smooth preforms
                                                                                       ′
on U of type (p, q) and (p′ , q′ ), respectively. Then g := (h, h′ ): U → Rn+n is a harmonic
tropicalization map, and one checks that (g, p1∗ α) = (h, α) and (g, p2∗ α′ ) = (h′ , α′ ). We
define the wedge product (h, α)∧(h′, α′ ) to be the weakly smooth preform (g, p1∗ α∧ p2∗ α′ )
                                                                         ′     ′
of type (p + p′ , q + q′ ). This satisfies (h′ , α′ ) ∧ (h, α) = (−1)(p+p )(q+q ) (h, α) ∧ (h′ , α′ ).
64                              W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

   We define differentials of preforms by d′ (h, α) = (h, d′ α) and d′′ (h, α) = (h, d′′ α). The
differentials satisfy the Leibniz rule
                                 
           d′ (h, α) ∧ (h′ , α′ ) = d′ (h, α) ∧ (h′ , α′ ) + (−1)(p+q)(h, α) ∧ d′′ (h′ , α′ ),
and likewise for d′′ . We conclude that the weakly smooth preforms form a presheaf of
bigraded differential R-algebras with respect to the differentials d′ and d′′ .
Definition 10.7 (Weakly smooth forms). The presheaf of weakly smooth preforms is
defined on the open subsets of X whose closure is a compact strictly analytic domain. We
denote the associated sheaf on the underlying topologicalL
                                                         space of X by AX or simply by
                                                                 p,q
A. It is a sheaf of bigraded differential R-algebras A =    p,q A . It is also a sheaf of
A -algebras. The elements of A are called weakly smooth forms. The elements A0,0 are
 0,0

well-defined real functions which we call weakly smooth functions.
10.8 (Pullback of Forms). For a morphism f : X ′ → X of good strictly analytic spaces,
there is a pull-back homomorphism f ∗ : AX → f∗ AX ′ of sheaves of bigraded differential
R-algebras, defined on preforms as follows: if U ⊂ X and U ′ ⊂ f −1 (U) are open subsets
whose closures are compact strictly analytic domains, and if (h, α) is a weakly smooth
preform on U, then f ∗ (h, α) := (h ◦ f , α).
  Let (h, α) be a weakly smooth preform on a compact good strictly analytic space X over
K. We denote the associated weakly smooth form by h∗ (α).
Proposition 10.9. Let X be a compact good strictly analytic space X over K and let h: X →
Rn be a harmonic tropicalization map.
   (1) The natural map from weakly smooth preforms to weakly smooth forms is injective.
   (2) The pullback h∗ : A•,• (h(X )) → A•,• (X ) is an injective homomorphism of bigraded
        differential R-algebras.
   (3) If f : X ′ → X is a morphism of good strictly analytic spaces, then (h ◦ f )∗ = f ∗ ◦ h∗ .
Proof. It is clear that (1) follows from (2) and Lemma 10.4, and (3) is obvious from
the definitions as h ◦ f is a harmonic tropicalization map by Remark 8.2. It remains to
show (2). Obviously, the map h∗ is a homomorphism of bigraded differential R-algebras.
To show the crucial injectivity, let us pick α ∈ A•,• (h(X )) with h∗ (α) = 0. Using that
the presheaf of weakly smooth forms and the sheaf of weakly smooth forms have the
same stalks, every x ∈ X has a compact strictly affinoid neighbourhood W such that
the preform (h|W , α|h(W ) ) is zero. By Lemma 10.4, we have α|h(W ) = 0. Using that X is
compact, we see that finitely many such W cover X and hence the corresponding h(W )
cover h(X ). We conclude that α = 0, proving injectivity in (2).                       
Remark 10.10 (Smooth forms). In the above, if we replace harmonic tropicalization
maps by smooth tropicalization maps, then we get the smooth differential forms intro-
duced Lby Chambert-Loir and Ducros (see below for a proof). They give rise to a sheaf
Asm = p,q Asm p,q
                  of bigraded differential R-algebras with respect to differentials d′ and d′′ .
The elements of A0,0 sm
                        are called smooth functions. Again, we have a functorial pull-back
with respect to morphisms of analytic spaces.
  Now we show that Asm agrees with the sheaf ACD of smooth forms defined in [CD12].
Let U ⊂ X be an open set whose closure U is a compact strictly analytic domain, and let
               FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                  65

(h, α) be a smooth preform on U as defined in 10.3 with respect to a smooth tropicaliza-
tion map h: U → Rn . Choose a moment map ϕ : U → Gm             n,an
                                                                     such that h = ϕtrop . Then
(ϕ|U , h(U)) is a tropical chart in the sense of [CD12, 3.1.1], so α ∈ A(h(U)) defines an
element of ACD (U), which does not depend on the choice of ϕ by Lemme 3.1.10 of loc.
cit. Sheafifying, we get a homomorphism of sheaves Asm → ACD . This homomorphism is
surjective by §3.1.9 of loc. cit., and it is injective by 10.9 and Lemme 3.2.2 of loc. cit.
Remark 10.11 (Piecewise smooth forms). In the above, if we replace harmonic tropical-
ization maps by smooth tropicalization maps and the sheaves AS of smooth Lagerberg
forms on the piecewise Z-linear spaces S by the sheaves AS,ps of piecewise smooth Lager-
berg forms (see 10.2), then we get a variant of the piecewise smooth     L differential   forms
                                                                                 p,q
introduced in [GK17, Section 9]. They give rise to a sheaf Aps = p,q Aps of bigraded
differential R-algebras with respect to differentials d′P and d′′P . The latter are called poly-
hedral derivatives; they do not coincide in general with the differentials of the associated
currents (this is a consequence of the tropical Poincaré–Lelong formula [GK17, Theo-
rem 0.1]). The elements of A0,0ps
                                  are called piecewise smooth functions. Again, we have a
functorial pull-back with respect to morphisms of analytic spaces.
Remark 10.12. Note that Lemma 10.4 and Proposition 10.9 hold also for smooth and
for piecewise smooth preforms. Indeed, the smooth case is just [CD12, Lemme 3.2.2],
and one readily checks that the argument works also in the piecewise smooth case.
10.13 (Relations between the forms). By Remark 8.2, every smooth tropicalization map
is harmonic, so we get a homomorphism Asm → A of sheaves of bigraded differential
R-algebras. We also have a canonical homomorphism A → Aps of sheaves of bigraded
differential R-algebras, defined as follows. Let (h, α) be a weakly smooth preform on an
open subset U of X whose closure U is a compact strictly analytic domain. We proved
in 8.4 that for any x ∈ U, there is a open neighbourhood U x of x whose closure is a
compact strictly analytic domain contained in U, a smooth tropicalization map h x on U x ,
and a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear map F : h′ (U x ) → h(U) such that h = F ◦ h x on U x . Since α
is a smooth Lagerberg form on h(U), we conclude that α x := F ∗ (α) is a piecewise smooth
form on h′ (U x ). One checks that the piecewise smooth preforms (h x , α x ) glue to a well-
defined piecewise smooth form on U, giving rise to the desired canonical homomorphism
A → Aps .
Proposition 10.14. The above canonical homomorphisms are injective: that is, we have
inclusions of sheaves of bigraded differential R-algebras
                                      Asm ,→ A ,→ Aps .
Proof. The map Asm → Aps is obviously injective and factors through A → Aps , so it
is enough to show that the homomorphism A → Aps is injective. Let U be an open
subset of X and let ω be weakly smooth form on U such that the associated piecewise
smooth form constructed in 10.13 is zero. Arguing locally, we may assume that U is a
compact strictly analytic domain and that ω is given on U by a weakly smooth preform
(h, α), where h: U → Rn is a harmonic tropicalization and α ∈ A(h(U)). Replacing U
by U x from 10.13, we may assume that h is covered by a smooth tropicalization map
66                             W. GUBLER, P. JELL, AND J. RABINOFF

h′ : U → Rm , i.e., there is a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear map F : h′ (U) → h(U) with h = F ◦ h′ ,
and that the piecewise smooth form (h′ )∗ (F ∗ α) on U is zero. Since Aps (h′ (U)) → Aps (U)
is injective, we have F ∗ α = 0. We conclude that there is a finite covering of h′ (U) by
(Z, Γ )-polytopes ∆′ maped (Z, Γ )-linearly by F onto polytopes ∆ covering h(U). Using
F ∗ α = 0, we conclude as in the proof of Lemma 10.4 that α = 0 on h(U). This proves
ω = 0, showing injectivity.                                                                 
Remark 10.15. The above result shows that weakly smooth forms are examples of piece-
wise smooth forms. We introduced the latter as they form a natural class for defining
integrals in the next section. As an example of a piecewise smooth function that is not
smooth, take any piecewise smooth function ϕ : R → R that is not smooth; then ϕ ◦ trop
is piecewise smooth but not smooth on Tan . A more sophisticated example of a function
which is harmonic but not smooth is given in Proposition 17.4.
   The piecewise smooth forms on X can be characterized as follows. A piecewise smooth
form ω is defined by a G-covering (Vi )i∈I by compact strictly analytic domains Vi and
smooth forms ωi on Vi such that ωi |Vi ∩Vj = ω j |Vi ∩Vj for all i, j ∈ I. This is proved as for
piecewise linear functions in Proposition 8.3.
10.16 (Supports). The support of ω ∈ A p,q (X ) is the set of points of X such that the germ
of ω in x is non-zero. This is a closed subset which we denote by supp(ω). We denote
by Acp,q the cosheaf of weakly smooth (p, q)-forms with compact support on X .
                             f(x)•/K
   If x ∈ X satisfies tr.deg(H        e • ) < max(p, q), then by [CD12, Lemme 3.2.5] we
have x ∈ / supp(ω) . This applies in particular to any point x contained in a Zariski-closed
subset of dimension less than max(p, q).
  Recall that the elements of A0,0 are called weakly smooth functions. It is easy to see
that they are continuous real functions.
Proposition 10.17. Let f : X → R be a function. The following are equivalent:
   (1) f is weakly smooth and d′ d′′ f = 0.
   (2) f is harmonic (and hence R-PL).
Proof. Assume first that f is harmonic (hence R-PL). By Proposition 7.5, we may shrink
X to assume that f is an R-linear combination of Z-harmonic functions. By linearity, we
may assume that f is itself Z-harmonic. Then we can use f as a harmonic tropicalization.
Using f = IdR ◦ f , we deduce that f ∈ A0,0 (X ) and d′ d′′ f = f ∗ (d′ d′′ IdR ) = 0.
   Conversely, let f be a weakly smooth function with d′ d′′ f = 0. We have to show that
f is a harmonic function. This can be checked locally at x ∈ X , so we may assume that X
is a strictly affinoid domain and that f is the composition of a harmonic tropicalization
h = (h1 , . . . , hn ): X → Rn with a smooth function g : Rn → R. We use the Taylor expansion
in ν := h(x) to write
                     g(ω) = g(ν) + a1 ω1 + · · · + an ωn + O(kω − νk2 )
for ω = (ω1 , . . . , ωn ) in a neighbourhood of ν. Using injectivity of h∗ from Proposi-
tion 10.9, we deduce from d′ d′′ h = 0 that d′ d′′ (g|h(X ) ) = 0, and hence that g is affine on
every face of the tropical variety h(X ). Thus the restrictions of g and of g(ν)+a1 ω1 +· · ·+
                FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                       67

an ωn to h(X ) agree in a neighbourhood of ν. We conclude that f = g(ν)+a1 h1 +· · ·+an hn
in a neighbourhood of x, and therefore f is a harmonic function.                        

11.4 (Lagerberg Involution). The Lagerberg involution is the unique involution J of the
sheaf A of R-algebras which leaves the smooth functions fixed and which satisfies Jd′ =
d′′ J. It maps A p,q isomorphically onto Aq,p . The Lagerberg involution is the reason that
the differentials d′ and d′′ is play a completely symmetric role in non-Archimedean ge-
ometry. We call ω ∈ A p,p (X ) symmetric if Jω = (−1) p ω.
Theorem 11.5 (Green’s formula). For symmetric forms ω ∈ Acp,p (X ) and η ∈ Aq,q
                                                                            c

p + q = d − 1, we have

                     X                                             ∂X

Proof. Similarly as in [CD12, Lemme 1.3.8], this follows from the theorem of Stokes
above and the Leibniz formula.                                                   
11.6 (Forms as Currents). Assume that X is Hausdorff and that ∂ X = ;. Then the space
Dsm (X ) of currents on X is defined and studied in [CD12, Section 4]. For ω ∈ Aps (X ),

for any η ∈ Asm,c (X ). We claim that the induced linear map Aps (X ) → Dsm (X ) is injective.
Indeed, it is elementary to check that the corresponding map from the space of piecewise
smooth Lagerberg forms to the space of Lagerberg currents on a piecewise (Z, Γ )-linear
space in Rn is injective. Then the claim follows from Remark 10.12.

   The d′ -residue of ω is defined as d′ [ω] − [d′ ω] and similarly we define the d′′ -residue
of ω. The theorem of Stokes shows that the d′ - and d′′ -residues of ω ∈ Ad−1,d−1 (X ) are
zero. In this sense, weakly smooth forms behave like smooth differential forms.

  We illustrate the theory of integration in a simple example explaining why it is nat-
ural to consider integration of piecewise smooth forms. Another reason to work in the
piecewise smooth setting is that harmonic functions and harmonic tropicalizations are
piecewise smooth, but not necessarily smooth as we will see in Remark 15.10.

   Most smooth functions f : X → R do not arise in this way. In general, the restriction

for g := min(u1 , 0); see [BGJK21b, Example 2.3.5].

              12. STRONG CURRENTS AND THE POINCARÉ–LELONG THEOREM
  In this section, we denote by X a Hausdorff strictly analytic space X of pure dimension
d with no boundary. Note that X is a good analytic space which is separated over K. We
have already seen that the sheaf A p,q of weakly smooth forms on X has many properties
                            p,q
analogous to its subsheaf Asm   of smooth forms. We now turn to the notion of currents,
               FORMS ON BERKOVICH SPACES BASED ON HARMONIC TROPICALIZATIONS                   71

                                        p,q
following [CD12, Section 4]. Since Asm,c    ⊂ Acp,q , we call the dual notion of currents
on Acp,q “strong currents” to distinguish them from the currents on Asm,cp,q
                                                                             in the sense
                                                                                  p,q
of Chambert-Loir and Ducros. Note that the restriction of a strong current to Asm,c   is a
current, hence strong currents can be seen as a generalization of currents.
12.1 (Topology on the Space of Forms). As in [CD12, 4.1.1], replacing smooth tropical-
ization maps by harmonic tropicalization maps, we define a (locally convex) topology
on the space Acp,q (X ) of compactly supported weakly smooth (p, q)-forms on X . Roughly
speaking, convergence means that the supports are covered by finitely many compact
strictly analytic subdomains Vi and the strictly analytic subdomains tropicalize the forms
such that all the derivatives of the corresponding Lagerberg forms converge uniformly.
Then the topology on the subspace of compactly supported smooth forms on X as defined
                                                                        p,q
in [CD12, 4.1.1] is just the subspace topology induced by the inlusion Asm,c (X ) ⊂ Acp,q (X ).
12.2 (Strong Currents). A strong current S on X of bidegree (p, q) is defined as a continu-
ous linear functional S : Acp,q (X ) → R. As usual we write 〈ω, S〉 = S(ω) for ω ∈ Acp,q (X ).
We denote by Dp,q (X ) the space of strong currents on X . Note that the restriction of S
      p,q
to Asm,c  (X ) is a current in the sense of [CD12, 4.2], i.e. we have a canonical linear map
Dp,q (X ) → Dsm     (X ).
                p,q       L
   We set D(X ) :=          p,q D p,q (X ). As in [CD12, 4.2], one shows that D (X ) is an A(X )-
module and that there is a direct image of currents with respect to proper morphisms.
The presheaf U 7→ D(U) of strong currents on open subsets U of X is a sheaf on X .
Example 12.3 (Current of Integration). The current of integration δX ∈ Dd,d (X ) is defined

for T ∈ Dp,q (X ), ω ∈ Acp−1,q (X ), and η ∈ Acp,q−1(X ). The sign is chosen such that the
